Completions of Restricted Complexity I Weak Arithmetical Theories
Ali Enayat, Mateusz Łełyk, Albert Visser

Abstract
. Given a first-order theory T T formulated in the usual language of first-order arithmetic, we say that T T is of restricted complexity if there is some natural number n n and some set 𝒜 \mathcal{A} of Σ n \Sigma_{n} -sentences such that T T can be axiomatized by 𝒜 \mathcal{A} . Motivated by the fact that no consistent arithmetical theory extending I ​ Δ 0 + 𝖤𝗑𝗉 \mathrm{I}\Delta_{0}+\mathsf{Exp} has a consistent completion that is of restricted complexity, we construct models of arithmetic whose complete theories are of restricted complexity. Our strongest result shows that there is a model of 𝖨𝖮𝗉𝖾𝗇 + 𝖢𝗈𝗅𝗅 \mathsf{IOpen+Coll} whose complete theory is of restricted complexity, where 𝖢𝗈𝗅𝗅 \mathsf{Coll} is the full collection scheme.



2010 Mathematics Subject Classification
03F25,03F30,03F40,

1.
Introduction

Given a first order theory
T
formulated in the usual language of first order arithmetic using the
signature
\{0,1,+,\times,<\}
, we say that
T
is of
restricted complexity
if there is some natural number
n

and some set
\mathcal{A}
of
\Sigma_{n}
-sentences such that
T
can be axiomatized by
\mathcal{A}
; here
\Sigma_{n}
refers
to the usual hierarchy of arithmetical formulæ, in which formulæ all of whose quantifiers are bounded are
classified as
\Sigma_{0}=\Delta_{0}
, and the other classes are obtained by prefixing
\Delta_{0}
-formulae with appropriate strings
of alternating blocks of quantifiers. Note that there are two independent ways to calibrate the complexity of an axiom set
\mathcal{A}

of arithmetical sentences: the complexity of the set of Gödel-numbers of
\mathcal{A}
versus the
\Sigma_{n}
-complexity of each
individual axiom in
\mathcal{A}
. It is the latter (and not the former) that is at stake here.

The point of departure for the work presented here is the following recent incompleteness result. A proof is given in
[
EV24
, Theorem B]
.
As pointed out in
[
EV24
]
, an abstract version of the result was formulated by Emil Jeřábek on MathOverflow in 2016.
We feel that such a fundamental insight should have been known much earlier, but we did not find it.

Theorem 1.1
.

Suppose
T
is a consistent theory formulated in the language of arithmetic such that
\mathrm{I}\Delta_{0}+\mathsf{Exp}
is provable in
T
. If
T
is of restricted complexity, then
T
is incomplete.

In the above,
\mathrm{I}\Delta_{0}
is the result of restricting the induction scheme in the usual axiomatization of
\mathsf{PA}
to
\Delta_{0}
-formulae, and
\mathsf{Exp}
is the sentence asserting the totality of the exponential function
x\mapsto 2^{x}
. Theorem
1.1
naturally prompts the following question:

Question 1.2
.

Let
T_{0}
be a consistent theory formulated in the language of
arithmetic that extends
\mathsf{Q}
(Robinson arithmetic) such that
\mathrm{I}\Delta_{0}+\mathsf{Exp}
is not provable in

T_{0}
.
Is there a consistent completion of
T_{0}
that is of restricted complexity?
1
1

1

It is known that Presburger arithmetic has a restricted axiomatization; this is a consequence of the elimination of quantifiers for Presburger arithmetic (see, for example, 4.5 on pp316, 317 and Exercise 9 on p328 in Craig Smoryński’s book
[
Smo91
]
). This also shows that Question 1.2 has an easy answer if we remove the condition that
T_{0}
includes
\mathsf{Q}
, since we can simply since we can add an axiom to Presburger arithmetic stipulating that the multiplication of any two numbers equals 0.

❍

For
T_{0}=\mathsf{I}\Delta_{0}
, the answer to Question
1.2
appears to be beyond
current methods, since the two different proofs of Theorem
1.1
as presented in
[
EV24
]
make it clear that the analogous result for
\mathsf{I}\Delta_{0}
implies that
\mathrm{I}\Delta_{0}
neither proves
that there is a
\Sigma_{1}
-satisfaction
predicate (which is known to be a tall order, as demonstrated by Zofia Adamowicz, Leszek Kołodziejczyk, and Jeff
Paris in
[
AKP12
]
), nor proves the MRDP theorem on the Diophantine representation of recursively enumerable sets (which is known to imply that NP = co-NP, as noted by Alex Wilkie
[
Wil80
]
).

In this paper, we show that Question
1.2
has a
positive answer

for certain well-known arithmetical theories
T_{0}
. Our strongest result provides a positive answer to Question
1.2
for
T_{0}=\mathsf{IOpen}+\mathsf{Coll}
, where
\mathsf{IOpen}
is the result of
restricting the induction scheme in the usual axiomatization of
\mathsf{PA}
to open
formulæ (i.e., quantifier-free arithmetical formulæ), and
\mathsf{Coll}
is the full scheme of collection.
2
2

2

This is to be contrasted with the well-known fact that
\mathrm{I}\Delta_{0}+\mathsf{Coll}
axiomatizes
\mathsf{PA}
. By careful inspection of the proof
of this last insight in
[
Kay91
, Lemma 7.4, p84]
, we see that this result
works already for the theory
\mathrm{I}{\sf E}_{1}
, which is, in a sense,

the next theory
above

IOpen
. See Appendix
B
for a brief exposition.

Our work in this project was set in motion upon obtaining a positive answer to Question
1.2
for
T_{0}=\mathsf{PA}^{-}
(
{\sf PA}^{-}
is the theory of the non-negative
part of an ordered commutative ring). More specifically we managed to demonstrate that the complete theory of the
model
\mathbb{Z}[{\sf X}]^{{\geqslant 0}}
of
\mathsf{PA}^{-}
is of restricted complexity.
Here
\mathbb{Z}[{\sf X}]^{{\geqslant 0}}
is the non-negative part of the ordered polynomial ring

\mathbb{Z}[{\sf X}]
, where
{\sf X}
is treated as a positive infinite element. At the macro level, the proof of Theorem
4.1
employs the
framework of interpretability theory, while at the micro level, it relies on sequentiality of
\mathsf{PA}^{-}

(demonstrated by Emil Jeřábek
[
Jeř12
]
) and
certain basic algebraic insights about
\mathbb{Z}[{\sf X}]^{{\geqslant 0}}
. This led us to obtain an analogous positive result for

T_{0}=\mathsf{IOpen}
with the same macro strategy as our first result. More specifically, we showed that the complete theory of
the model of
\mathsf{IOpen}
built by John Shepherdson using Puiseux series
in his ground-breaking paper
[
She64
]
is of restricted complexity.

Let
\mathsf{Coll}
be the full scheme of collection. The interpretability framework used to settle Question
1.2
for
T_{0}=\mathsf{PA}^{-}
and
T_{0}=\mathsf{IOpen}
employ definable injections of the universe into bounded range, and therefore a different strategy is needed for
T_{0}=\mathsf{PA}^{-}+\mathsf{Coll}
and
T_{0}=\mathsf{IOpen}+\mathsf{Coll}
, since such definable injections are ruled out by
\mathsf{Coll}
. This led us to formulate a modified interpretability framework in which the notion of isomorphism is replaced with the notion of
partial isomorphism
(in the back-and-forth style of Ehrenfeucht-Fraïssé
games). We used this new framework at the macro level, together with refined algebraic book-keeping arguments at the micro level to establish a positive answer to Question
1.2
for
T_{0}=\mathsf{PA}^{-}+\mathsf{Coll}
using the model
\mathbb{Z}[\,{\sf X}_{q}\mathop{\mid}q\in\mathbb{Q}]^{{\geqslant 0}}
of
\mathsf{PA}^{-}+\mathsf{Coll}
that was recently introduced in

[
EL24
]
(here
\mathbb{Q}
is the ordered set of rationals). This advance, in turn, inspired us to successfully apply the aforementioned modified interpretability framework based on partial isomorphisms, together with the necessary algebraic considerations, to build a model of
\mathsf{IOpen}+\mathsf{Coll}
whose complete theory is of restricted complexity. Intuitively speaking, the relevant model is obtained by iterating the aforementioned Shepherdson construction
\mathbb{Q}
-times (as a direct limit). It is noteworthy that in all the models analyzed in this paper whose complete theory is of restricted complexity, the witnessing set
\mathcal{A}
of
\Sigma_{n}
-sets consists of a single complex sentence plus an infinite set of
\Sigma_{1}
-sentences.
3
3

3

With more effort, these completions can be arranged to be also computably as simple as incompleteness allows: for each of the constructions in this paper, we can perform the same construction within a model of
{\sf PA}
whose elementary diagram is
\Delta_{2}
(instead of performing the construction within the standard model of
{\sf PA}
), thereby obtaining a restricted completion which is additionally
\Delta_{2}
as a set of natural numbers. Moreover, by taking advantage of Craig’s trick, such a
\Delta_{2}
completion can be axiomatized by a collection of axioms
\mathcal{A}
such that
\mathcal{A}
is
\Pi_{1}
as a set of natural numbers.

There is a parallelism between the following three projects and our work here in constructing models of arithmetical theories whose theories are of restricted complexity.

1
.

The construction of recursive (computable) nonstandard models of arithmetical theories, a topic boasting a large literature that originated with Shepherdson’s pioneering paper
[
She64
]
(which came on the heels of Tennenbaum’s classical theorem on the nonexistence of computable models of Peano Arithmetic). Shahram Mohsenipour’s
[
Moh06
]
includes the latest known results and key references.

2
.

The determination of the arithmetical theories in which MRDP is provable. Here by MRDP we refer to the abstract version of the Matijasevič-Robinson-Davis-Putnam theorem on the Diophantine representability of computably enumerable sets that states that every
\Sigma_{1}
-formula is provably equivalent to an existential formula, as in Richard Kaye’s
[
Kay11
]
. A recent contribution in this direction (and the one below) is by Yijia Chen, Moritz Müller and Keita Yokoyama
[
CMY24
]
.

3
.

Delimiting arithmetical theories that support a definable truth-predicate for
\Delta_{0}
-formulae. A key paper exploring this topic is
[
AKP12
]
, which was mentioned earlier (right after Question
1.2
); a more recent contribution in this direction is by Konrad Zdanowski
[
Zda21
]
.

In order to elaborate on the aforementioned parallelism, consider the set
\mathcal{T}_{\mathsf{Q}}
of consistent theories formulated in the
language of arithmetic that extend
\mathsf{Q}
, and consider the following subclasses of theories
\mathcal{T}_{\mathsf{Q}}
:

\bullet

{\mathcal{T}}_{\mathrm{MRDP}}
is the set of
T\in\mathcal{T}_{\mathsf{Q}}
such that MRDP is provable in
T
.

\bullet

{\mathcal{T}}_{\mathrm{truth}}
is the set of
T\in\mathcal{T}_{\mathsf{Q}}
such that
T
supports a definable truth-predicate for
\Delta_{0}
-formulae.

\bullet

{\mathcal{T}}_{\mathrm{nrec}}
is the set of
T\in\mathcal{T}_{\mathsf{Q}}
such that
T
does not have a nonstandard recursive (computable) model.

\bullet

{\mathcal{T}}_{\mathrm{nrestr}}
is the set of
T\in\mathcal{T}_{\mathsf{Q}}
such that no consistent completion of
T
is of restricted complexity.

As shown by Costas Dimitracopoulos and Haim Gaifman
[
GD82
]
all arithmetical theories that extend
\mathrm{I}\Delta_{0}+\mathsf{Exp}
are members of
{\mathcal{T}}_{\mathrm{truth}}\cap{\mathcal{T}}_{\mathrm{MRDP}}
. On the other hand, Kaye
[
Kay11
, Theorem 3.3]
, showed that
{\mathcal{T}}_{\mathrm{MRDP}}\subseteq{\mathcal{T}}_{\mathrm{nrec}}
. The two proofs of
[
EV24
, Theorem B]
show that
{\mathcal{T}}_{\mathrm{truth}}\subseteq T_{\mathrm{nrestr}}
, and
{\mathcal{T}}_{\mathrm{MRDP}}\subseteq T_{\mathrm{nrestr}}
. This is to be contrasted with the strongest result in this paper that shows that
\mathsf{IOpen+Coll}\notin{\mathcal{T}}_{\mathrm{nrestr}}
.
Constructing separating examples that distinguish the three classes
T_{\mathrm{MRDP}}
,
T_{\mathrm{truth}}
, and
{\mathcal{T}}_{\mathrm{nrec}}
from each other appears to be a long-standing open question. We do not know whether the construction of examples that separate the class
{\mathcal{T}}_{\mathrm{nrestr}}
from the aforementioned three is within reach.

A word about the organization of the paper is in order. Section 2 is devoted to relevant preliminaries concerning the arithmetical theories considered in this paper (Subsection 2.1), interpretability theory (Subsection 2.2), and salient algebraic facts (Subsection 2.3). Section 3 is short but very important since it sets up the interpretability framework for all the constructions of the paper. In Section 4, we show that the theory of
\mathbb{Z}[{\sf X}]^{{\geqslant 0}}
is of restricted complexity, and in Section 5 we do the same for Shepherdson’s model of
\mathsf{IOpen}
. In Section 5, we show that the theory of the model
\mathbb{Z}[\,{\sf X}_{q}\mathop{\mid}q\in\mathbb{Q}]^{{\geqslant 0}}
of
\mathsf{PA}^{-}+\mathsf{Coll}
is of restricted complexity. At the technical level this section is considerably more involved than
Sections 3 and 4 due to the complexity of the salient algebraic ideas and the nuances of the modified interpretability framework based on partial isomorphisms.
Section 5 can be seen as a dress rehearsal for Section 6 in which we establish the culminating result of the paper by exhibiting a model of
\mathsf{IOpen}+\mathsf{Collection}
whose theory is of restricted complexity.

2.
Basics

2.1.
Theories

All our theories are theories of predicate logic of finite signature.
We write
{\sf Th}_{\varTheta}
for the deductive closure of the axioms of predicate logic of signature
\varTheta
.

For the purposes of this paper, an
extensional
view of theories suffices.
So, a theory will be given by a signature and a deductively closed set of sentences of that
signature.

2.2.
{\sf PA}^{-}
and Extensions

In this paper we are specifically interested in the theory
{\sf PA}^{-}
and weak theories extending it.
{\sf PA}^{-}
is the theory of the non-negative parts of discretely ordered
commutative rings. The signature of our theory is the signature with 0, 1,
+
,
\times
,
<
. We will follow the usual notational conventions for omitting brackets and in some cases
omitting
\times
writing, e.g.,
xy
for
x\times y
.
Here are the axioms of the theory as given by Richard Kaye in
[
Kay91
, Chapter 2.1]
.

pam
1.

\vdash(x+y)+z=x+(y+z)

pam
2.

\vdash x+y=y+x

pam
3.

\vdash(x\times y)\times z=x\times(y\times z)

pam
4.

\vdash x\times y=y\times x

pam
5.

\vdash x\times(y+z)=x\times y+x\times z

pam
6.

\vdash x+0=x\wedge x\times 0=0

pam
7.

\vdash x\times 1=x

pam
8.

\vdash(x<y\wedge y<z)\to x<z

pam
9.

\vdash\neg\,x<x

pam
10.

\vdash x<y\vee x=y\vee y<x

pam
11.

\vdash x<y\to x+z<y+z

pam
12.

\vdash(0<z\wedge x<y)\to x\times z<x\times y

pam
13.

\vdash x<y\to\exists z\;x+z=y

pam
14.

\vdash 0<1\wedge(0<x\to(x=1\vee 1<x))

pam
15.

\vdash x=0\vee 0<x

The theory
{\sf PA}^{-}
turns out to be a good base for weak arithmetical theories. We note that all axioms except
pam
13
are universal; the system without this substraction axiom, sometimes jokingly called
{\sf PA}^{{---}}
(
{\sf PA}^{-}
-minus-minus), was studied by Emil Jeřábek in
[
Jeř12
]
.
Jeřábek verifies that we have a good sequence coding in this theory, i.o.w., that this
theory is sequential. For detailed information on sequential
theories, see
[
Vis13
]
. Sequentiality of a theory is inherited by its extensions. Thus, all theories studied in the present paper have the property.

We briefly present some of Jeřábek’s results for
{\sf PA}^{-}
, noting that they already hold in the weaker theory without subtraction.
First, Jeřábek proves that
{\langle x,y\rangle}:=(x+y)^{2}+x
is a pairing function in
{\sf PA}^{-}
.
We note that, in
{\sf PA}^{-}
, we have
x\leqslant{\langle x,y\rangle}
and
y\leqslant{\langle x,y\rangle}
.
Then, Jeřábek defines his version of the
\beta
-function:

\bullet

\beta(x,i,w):\leftrightarrow\exists u,v,q\,(w={\langle u,v\rangle}\wedge u=q(1+(i+1)v)+x\wedge x\leqslant(1+(i+1)v))
.

Clearly,
u
,
v
and
q
can be bounded by
w
. So, this gives us a
\Delta_{0}
-definition.
Also, necessarily
x\leqslant w
.

[
Jeř12
, Lemma 4(iii)]
tells us that
\beta
is functional from
i,w
to
x
.

We define:

\bullet

{\sf seq}^{\ast}(s):\leftrightarrow\exists n,w\leqslant s\,(s={\langle n,w\rangle}\wedge\forall i\mathbin{<}n\,\exists x\mathbin{\leqslant}w\,\beta(x,i,w))
.

In case
s
is an
\ast
-sequence (i.e.,
\mathsf{seq}^{\ast}(s)
holds),
Clearly, the length function has a
\Delta_{0}
-graph. We also write
\pi(s,i)=x
, whenever
s
is an
\ast
-sequence
{\langle n,w\rangle}
,
i<n
, and

\beta(x,i,w)
. We note that the graph of
\pi
is
\Delta_{0}
and
\pi
is total on
i<n
, whenever
s
is an
\ast
-sequence.

A crucial result is
[
Jeř12
, Lemma 8]
. In our terminology, this says that, in
{\sf PA}^{-}
, for a certain definable inductive
J
, we have: (†)
for all
\ast
-sequences
s
where the length of
s
is
n
in
J
and for all
x
, there is an
\ast
-sequence
s^{\prime}
of length
n+1
, such that

\pi(s,i)=\pi(s^{\prime},i)
for all
i<n
and
\pi(s^{\prime},n)=x
.

In the nonstandard models of
{\sf PA}^{-}
we will consider, the standard cut
\omega
is
\Delta_{0}
-definable, say by
\eta
. The property given by (†) is clearly inherited by definable inductive
J^{\prime}

that are shorter than
J
. Since
\omega
is initial in any inductive
J^{\prime}
, we will have (†) for
\omega
as defined by
\eta
.
This inspires the following definition:
{\sf seq}(s)
iff
{\sf length}(s)\in\eta
and
{\sf seq}^{\ast}(s)
.
Thus,
seq
will deliver, inside
\mathcal{M}
, sequences in the usual sense.

The theory
{\sf PA}^{-}
and the theory of discretely ordered commutative rings have a close connection. We will give an explication of this connection in
Example
2.1
.

We will also consider the following extensions of
{\sf PA}^{-}
.

\bullet

\mathsf{IOpen}
(
Open Induction
). This is the extension of
{\sf PA}^{-}
obtained by augmenting
{\sf PA}^{-}
with the induction scheme for
quantifier-free formulas
(parameters allowed).

\bullet

\mathsf{PA}^{-}+\mathsf{Coll}
, where
\mathsf{Coll}
is the
collection scheme
consisting of formulae of the
following form in which parameters are allowed:

\forall x\mathbin{<}v\,\exists y\;\varphi(x,y,\mathchoice{\vbox{\halign{#\cr\kern-0.7pt\cr$\mkern \bullet

\mathsf{IOpen}+{\sf Coll}
.

2.3.
Basics of Interpretations

In this section, we briefly sketch the basics of interpretations.

2.3.1.
Translations and Interpretations

We present the notion of
m
-dimensional interpretation without parameters
.
There are two extensions of this notion: we can consider piecewise interpretations
and we can add parameters. We will not treat the piecewise case, but will briefly indicate how to
add parameters.

Consider two signatures
\Sigma
and
\varTheta
. An
m
-dimensional translation
\tau:\Sigma\to\varTheta

is a quadruple
{\langle\Sigma,\delta,\mathcal{F},\varTheta\rangle}
, where
\delta(v_{0},\ldots,v_{m-1})
is a
\varTheta
-formula and where,
for any
n
-ary predicate
P
of
\Sigma
,
\mathcal{F}(P)
is a
\varTheta
-formula
\varphi(\mathchoice{\vbox{\halign{#\cr\kern-0.7pt\cr$\mkern 2.0mu\displaystyle\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}}\mkern-1.5mu\leaders{\hbox{$\displaystyle\mkern in the language
of signature

, where

\mathchoice{\vbox{\halign{#\cr\kern-0.7pt\cr$\mkern .
Both in the case of
\delta
and
\varphi
all free variables are among the variables shown.
Moreover, if
i\neq j
or
k\neq\ell
, then
v_{i,k}
is syntactically different from
v_{j,\ell}
.

Here

is provability in predicate logic. This demand is inessential, but it is convenient to have.

\psi^{\tau}
as follows:

commutes with the propositional connectives.

There are two worries about this definition.
First, what variables
\mathchoice{\vbox{\halign{#\cr\kern-0.7pt\cr$\mkern 2.0mu\displaystyle\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}}\mkern-1.5mu\leaders{\hbox{$\displaystyle\mkern 2.0mu\textstyle\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}}\mkern-1.5mu\leaders{\hbox{$\textstyle\mkern 0.0mu$}}{\hfill}\mkern-1.5mu\fldr$\crcr\kern-0.3pt\cr$\hfil\textstyle x\hfil$\crcr}}}{\vbox{\halign{#\cr\kern-0.7pt\cr$\mkern 2.0mu\scriptstyle\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}}\mkern-1.5mu\leaders{\hbox{$\scriptstyle\mkern 0.0mu\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\montraita}$}}}\mkern 0.0mu$}}{\hfill}\mkern-1.5mu\fldr$\crcr\kern-0.3pt\cr$\hfil\scriptstyle x\hfil$\crcr}}}{\vbox{\halign{#\cr\kern-0.7pt\cr$\mkern 2.0mu\scriptscriptstyle\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}}\mkern-1.5mu\leaders{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\mkern 0.0mu\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\montraita}$}}}\mkern 0.0mu$}}{\hfill}\mkern-1.5mu\fldr$\crcr\kern-0.3pt\cr$\hfil\scriptscriptstyle x\hfil$\crcr}}}_{i}
on the side of the translation
A^{\tau}

correspond with
x_{i}
in the original formula
A
?
The second worry is that substitution of variables in
\delta

and
\mathcal{F}(P)
may cause variable-clashes.
These worries are never important in practice: we choose
‘suitable’ sequences

\mkern 2.0mu\textstyle\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}}\mkern-1.5mu\leaders{\hbox{$\textstyle\mkern 0.0mu\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\montraita}$}}}\mkern 0.0mu$}}{\hfill}\mkern-1.5mu\fldr

\hfil\textstyle x\hfil

to correspond to variables
x
, and we avoid clashes by re-naming bound variables, i.e., we apply
\alpha
-conversion.
However, if we want to give precise definitions of translations and, for example, of composition of translations, these
problems come into play. The problems are clearly solvable in a systematic way, but this endeavor is beyond the scope of this paper.

We allow the identity predicate to be translated to a formula that is not identity.

A translation
\tau
is
identity preserving
if it translates identity to identity, i.e., if
(x=x)^{\tau}=(x=x)
. A translation is
unrelativised

if its domain is the full domain of the theory, i.e., if
\delta_{\tau}(x)=(x=x)
.
It is
direct
, if it is one-dimensional and identity preserving and unrelativised.

There are several important operations on translations.

\bullet

{\sf id}_{\Sigma}
is the identity translation. We take
\delta_{{\sf id}_{\Sigma}}(v):=v=v
and
\mathcal{F}(P):=P(\mathchoice{\vbox{\halign{#\cr\kern-0.7pt\cr$\mkern 2.0mu\displaystyle\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}}\mkern-1.5mu\leaders{\hbox{$\displaystyle\mkern 0.0mu\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\montraita}$}}}\mkern 0.0mu$}}{\hfill}\mkern-1.5mu\fldr$\crcr\kern-0.3pt\cr$\hfil\displaystyle v\hfil$\crcr}}}{\vbox{\halign{#\cr\kern-0.7pt\cr$\mkern 2.0mu\textstyle\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}}\mkern-1.5mu\leaders{\hbox{$\textstyle\mkern 0.0mu\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\montraita}$}}}\mkern 0.0mu$}}{\hfill}\mkern-1.5mu\fldr$\crcr\kern-0.3pt\cr$\hfil\textstyle v\hfil$\crcr}}}{\vbox{\halign{#\cr\kern-0.7pt\cr$\mkern 2.0mu\scriptstyle\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}}\mkern-1.5mu\leaders{\hbox{$\scriptstyle\mkern 0.0mu\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\montraita}$}}}\mkern 0.0mu$}}{\hfill}\mkern-1.5mu\fldr$\crcr\kern-0.3pt\cr$\hfil\scriptstyle v\hfil$\crcr}}}{\vbox{\halign{#\cr\kern-0.7pt\cr$\mkern 2.0mu\scriptscriptstyle\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}}\mkern-1.5mu\leaders{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\mkern .

\bullet

We can compose translations. Suppose
\tau:\Sigma\to\varTheta
and
\nu:\varTheta\to\Lambda
.
Then
\nu\circ\tau
or
\tau\nu
is a translation from
\Sigma
to
\Lambda
. We define:

.

A translation relates signatures; an interpretation relates theories.
An interpretation
\imath:U\to V
is a triple
{\langle U,\tau,V\rangle}
, where
U
and
V
are theories and
\tau:\Sigma_{U}\to\Sigma_{V}
.
We demand: for all theorems
\varphi
of
U
, we have
V\vdash\varphi^{\tau}
. In a context where we have an interpretation
\imath
based on
\tau
, we will often write

\varphi^{\imath}
for
\varphi^{\tau}
.

Here are some further definitions.

\bullet

{\sf ID}_{U}:U\to U
is the interpretation
{\langle U,{\sf id}_{\Sigma_{U}},U\rangle}
.

\bullet

Suppose
\imath:U\to V
and
\jmath:V\to W
. Then,
\imath\jmath:=\jmath\circ\imath:U\to W
is
{\langle U,\tau_{\jmath}\circ\tau_{\imath},W\rangle}
.

A translation
\tau
maps a model
\mathcal{M}
to an internal model
\widetilde{\tau}(\mathcal{M})
provided that

\mathcal{M}
satisfies the
\tau
-translations of the identity axioms (including
\exists x\,x=x
). Thus, an interpretation
\imath:U\to V
gives us a
mapping
\widetilde{\imath}
from
{\sf MOD}(V)
, the class of models of
V
, to
{\sf MOD}(U)
, the class
of models of
U
. If we build a category of theories and interpretations, usually
MOD

with
{\sf MOD}(\imath):=\widetilde{\imath}
will be a contravariant functor.

We can extend our notion of interpretation by adding a parameter-domain
\alpha(\mathchoice{\vbox{\halign{#\cr\kern-0.7pt\cr$\mkern 2.0mu\displaystyle\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}}\mkern-1.5mu\leaders{\hbox{$\displaystyle\mkern 0.0mu\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\montraita}$}}}\mkern 0.0mu$}}{\hfill}\mkern-1.5mu\fldr$\crcr\kern-0.3pt\cr$\hfil\displaystyle v\hfil$\crcr}}}{\vbox{\halign{#\cr\kern-0.7pt\cr$\mkern 2.0mu\textstyle\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}}\mkern-1.5mu\leaders{\hbox{$\textstyle\mkern 0.0mu\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\montraita}$}}}\mkern 0.0mu$}}{\hfill}\mkern-1.5mu\fldr$\crcr\kern-0.3pt\cr$\hfil\textstyle v\hfil$\crcr}}}{\vbox{\halign{#\cr\kern-0.7pt\cr$\mkern 2.0mu\scriptstyle\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}}\mkern-1.5mu\leaders{\hbox{$\scriptstyle\mkern 0.0mu\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\montraita}$}}}\mkern 0.0mu$}}{\hfill}\mkern-1.5mu\fldr$\crcr\kern-0.3pt\cr$\hfil\scriptstyle v\hfil$\crcr}}}{\vbox{\halign{#\cr\kern-0.7pt\cr$\mkern . The dimension of this domain is
unrelated to the dimension of the corresponding translation. The variables

\mkern 2.0mu\textstyle\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}}\mkern-1.5mu\leaders{\hbox{$\textstyle\mkern 0.0mu\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\montraita}$}}}\mkern 0.0mu$}}{\hfill}\mkern-1.5mu\fldr

\hfil\textstyle v\hfil

of the parameter domain are admitted
as parameters in the translations for domain and predicate symbols (including identity). We demand that the interpreting theory
proves that the parameter domain is non-empty.
We say that
\imath={\langle U,\tau,\alpha,V\rangle}
is an interpretation if, for all sentences
\varphi
of the signature of

U
, if
U\vdash\varphi
, then

V\vdash\forall\mathchoice{\vbox{\halign{#\cr\kern-0.7pt\cr$\mkern .

We write
U^{\tau}
for
\{\varphi^{\tau}\mathop{\mid}U\vdash\varphi\}
.
We note that if
A
is finitely axiomatisable, then so is
A^{\tau}
. Suppose
\varphi
is a single axiom for

A
. We axiomatise
A^{\tau}
by
\varphi^{\tau}
plus the
\tau
-translations of the identity axioms for
the signature of
A
. These axioms include
\exists x\,x=x
ensuring the non-emptiness of the domain.
The addition of the translations of the identity axioms is necessitated, in many cases, by the
translation being relativised and the fact that identity does not translate to identity.

We note that, trivially, we have an interpretation
\imath
of
U
in
U^{\tau}
based on translation
\tau
.
In fact
U^{\tau}
is the minimal theory
V
such that there is an interpretation of
U
in
V
based on
\tau
.

In case our translation has parameters with parameter domain
\alpha
, the theory
U^{\tau}
has to contain

\exists\mathchoice{\vbox{\halign{#\cr\kern-0.7pt\cr$\mkern , whenever
U\vdash\varphi
.

2.3.2.
Categories of Theories and Interpretations

We introduce some relevant categories of theories and interpretations. The objects of these categories are theories
considered as sets of theorems
.
We already introduced identity interpretations and composition of
interpretations, however these do not yet yield a category since e.g.
{\sf ID}_{V}\circ\imath=\imath
fails (where
\imath:U\to V
). The solution is to
define an appropriate notion of sameness of interpretation. It turns out that there are quite different choices of these notions of sameness which lead
a.o. to quite different isomorphisms between theories. This idea was studied in
[
Vis06
]
for the one-dimensional case. In the present paper,
we will need two new such categories. The following categories are well-known.

\bullet

{\sf INT}_{0}
is the category where two interpretations
\imath,\jmath:U\to V
are
the same if
V
proves that the domains provided by both interpretations are the same and that the translations of the predicate symbols of
V
are the same on these domains.
Isomorphism between theories in this category is
definitional equivalence
or
synonymy
.

\bullet

{\sf INT}_{1}
is the category where two interpretations
\imath,\jmath:U\to V
are
the same if there is a
V
-definable,
V
-verifiable isomorphism
F
between
\imath
and
\jmath
.
Isomorphism in this category is
bi-interpretability
.

\bullet

{\sf INT}_{2}
is the category where two interpretations
\imath,\jmath:U\to V
are
the same if, for all
V
-models
\mathcal{M}
, the internal models
\mathcal{M}^{\imath}
and

\mathcal{M}^{\jmath}
are isomorphic. Isomorphism in this category is
iso-congruence
.

\bullet

{\sf INT}_{3}
is the category where two interpretations
\imath,\jmath:U\to V
are
the same if, for all
U
-sentences
\varphi
, we have
V\vdash\varphi^{\imath}\leftrightarrow\varphi^{\jmath}
.
Isomorphism in this category is
elementary congruence
or
sentential congruence
.

Note that the above categories involve parameter-free interpretations, but they readily can be adapted to the parametric context.

We note that, e.g., in
{\sf INT}_{1}
,
there are various choices on how to set up the case of parameters.
Moreover, in the parametric
{\sf INT}_{1}
-case, the definable isomorphism

F
may contain extra parameters; in particular
F
will be allowed to have parameters even if
\imath

and
\jmath
are parameter-free. This will be the case for our definable isomorphisms in
Sections
4
and
5
).

Since ‘
F_{x}
is an isomorphism’, where
x
is the parameter, can be presented as a single
formula, we can take the virtual class of
x
such that
F_{x}
is an isomorphism as parameter domain for

F
. The only thing we need to verify is that there is an
x
such that
F_{x}
is an isomorphism.
So, in a sense, we can do without parameter domain.
4
4

4

This is analogous to the treatment of parameters
for interpretations of finitely axiomatized theories in
[
MPS90
, p3]
.
Our treatment
in Sections
4
and
5
) will follow this idea.

Example 2.1
.

Consider
{\sf PA}^{-}
and the theory of discretely ordered commutative rings, denoted
Docr
.
We interpret
{\sf PA}^{-}
in
Docr
simply by domain restriction, and we interpret
Docr
in
{\sf PA}^{-}
, e.g., by
taking as domain the odd-or-evens and by
letting
2n
pose as
n
and
2n+1
as
-n-1
.
5
5

5

Alternatively, we can use the pair
{\langle 0,n\rangle}
to represent the number
n
and the pair
{\langle 1,n\rangle}
to represent
-n-1
.

These two interpretations form a bi-interpretation.
Using the main result of
[
FV25
]
, we can use this bi-interpretation to construct a
definitional equivalence.

We introduce two new categories. We treat the one-dimensional parameter-free case.

The first,
{\sf INT}_{3}^{\sf fw}
or

{\sf INT}_{2{\tt b}}
, is the category of
finitely witnessed elementary equivalence. Here two interpretations
\imath,\jmath:U\to V

are the same if there is a finite sub-theory
V_{0}
of
V
, such that
or all
U
-sentences
\varphi
, we have
V_{0}\vdash\varphi^{\imath}\leftrightarrow\varphi^{\jmath}
.
It is easy to see that this gives indeed a category.
Suppose
V_{0}
witnesses the sameness of
\imath_{0},\imath_{1}:U\to V
and
W_{0}
witnesses the
sameness of
\jmath_{0},\jmath_{1}:V\to W
.
Then:

\displaystyle W_{0}+V_{0}^{\jmath_{0}}\vdash\varphi^{\imath_{0}\jmath_{0}}

\displaystyle\leftrightarrow

\displaystyle\varphi^{\imath_{1}\jmath_{0}}

\displaystyle\leftrightarrow

\displaystyle\varphi^{\imath_{1}\jmath_{1}}

Clearly,
{\sf INT}_{3}^{\sf fw}
is between
{\sf INT}_{3}
and
{\sf INT}_{1}
. It
is
prima facie
incomparable with
{\sf INT}_{2}
.
Isomorphism in this category is
finitely witnessed elementary congruence
or
finitely witnessed sentential congruence
.

The second new category
{\sf INT}_{2{\tt a}}
is the category where sameness
between
K,M:U\to V
is witnessed by an Ehrenfeucht-Fraïssé game. We give the definition
for the one-dimensional case without parameters. To get the definition off the
ground we need to assume that
V
is sequential. So, the reasonable thing to
do is to demand that all theories in the category are sequential.
Thus, the correct name of our category really should be
{\sf INT}^{\sf seq}_{2{\tt a}}
.
The Ehrenfeucht-Fraïssé game is given by a unary predicate
\eta
such that the following properties
are
V
-verifiable.

\bullet

If
f\in\eta
then
f
represents a partial finite bijection between
\delta_{\imath}
and
\delta_{\jmath}
.
Specifically, if
fxy
and
fx^{\prime}y^{\prime}
, then
x,x^{\prime}\in\delta_{\imath}
and
y,y^{\prime}\in\delta_{\jmath}
and (
x=_{\imath}x^{\prime}
iff
y=_{\jmath}y^{\prime})
.

\bullet

Suppose
P
is a
U
-predicate,
f\in\eta
, and

\mkern 2.0mu\textstyle\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}}\mkern-1.5mu\leaders{\hbox{$\textstyle\mkern 0.0mu\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\montraita}$}}}\mkern 0.0mu$}}{\hfill}\mkern-1.5mu\fldr

\hfil\textstyle x\hfil

is in the domain of
f
.
Then,
P_{\imath}(\mathchoice{\vbox{\halign{#\cr\kern-0.7pt\cr$\mkern 2.0mu\displaystyle\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}}\mkern-1.5mu\leaders{\hbox{$\displaystyle\mkern 0.0mu\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\montraita}$}}}\mkern 0.0mu$}}{\hfill}\mkern-1.5mu\fldr$\crcr\kern-0.3pt\cr$\hfil\displaystyle x\hfil$\crcr}}}{\vbox{\halign{#\cr\kern-0.7pt\cr$\mkern 2.0mu\textstyle\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}}\mkern-1.5mu\leaders{\hbox{$\textstyle\mkern 0.0mu\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\montraita}$}}}\mkern 0.0mu$}}{\hfill}\mkern-1.5mu\fldr$\crcr\kern-0.3pt\cr$\hfil\textstyle x\hfil$\crcr}}}{\vbox{\halign{#\cr\kern-0.7pt\cr$\mkern 2.0mu\scriptstyle\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}}\mkern-1.5mu\leaders{\hbox{$\scriptstyle\mkern 0.0mu\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\montraita}$}}}\mkern 0.0mu$}}{\hfill}\mkern-1.5mu\fldr$\crcr\kern-0.3pt\cr$\hfil\scriptstyle x\hfil$\crcr}}}{\vbox{\halign{#\cr\kern-0.7pt\cr$\mkern 2.0mu\scriptscriptstyle\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}}\mkern-1.5mu\leaders{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\mkern 0.0mu\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\montraita}$}}}\mkern 0.0mu$}}{\hfill}\mkern-1.5mu\fldr$\crcr\kern-0.3pt\cr$\hfil\scriptscriptstyle x\hfil$\crcr}}})
iff
P_{\jmath}(f\mathchoice{\vbox{\halign{#\cr\kern-0.7pt\cr$\mkern 2.0mu\displaystyle\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}}\mkern-1.5mu\leaders{\hbox{$\displaystyle\mkern 0.0mu\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\montraita}$}}}\mkern 0.0mu$}}{\hfill}\mkern-1.5mu\fldr$\crcr\kern-0.3pt\cr$\hfil\displaystyle x\hfil$\crcr}}}{\vbox{\halign{#\cr\kern-0.7pt\cr$\mkern 2.0mu\textstyle\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}}\mkern-1.5mu\leaders{\hbox{$\textstyle\mkern 0.0mu\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\montraita}$}}}\mkern 0.0mu$}}{\hfill}\mkern-1.5mu\fldr$\crcr\kern-0.3pt\cr$\hfil\textstyle x\hfil$\crcr}}}{\vbox{\halign{#\cr\kern-0.7pt\cr$\mkern 2.0mu\scriptstyle\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}}\mkern-1.5mu\leaders{\hbox{$\scriptstyle\mkern 0.0mu\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\montraita}$}}}\mkern 0.0mu$}}{\hfill}\mkern-1.5mu\fldr$\crcr\kern-0.3pt\cr$\hfil\scriptstyle x\hfil$\crcr}}}{\vbox{\halign{#\cr\kern-0.7pt\cr$\mkern 2.0mu\scriptscriptstyle\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}}\mkern-1.5mu\leaders{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\mkern 0.0mu\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\montraita}$}}}\mkern 0.0mu$}}{\hfill}\mkern-1.5mu\fldr$\crcr\kern-0.3pt\cr$\hfil\scriptscriptstyle x\hfil$\crcr}}})
.

\bullet

Suppose
f\in\eta
and
x\in\delta_{\imath}
and
x
is not
\imath
-equal to an element in the domain of
f
. Then, there is a
g\in\eta
with

\forall z\,\bigl(z\in{\sf dom}(g)\leftrightarrow(z\in{\sf dom}(f)\vee z=x)\bigr)

and
\forall z\in{\sf dom}(f)\;f(z)=g(z)
.
(Note that two different
f
can code the same input-output relation.)

\bullet

Suppose
f\in\eta
and
y\in\delta_{\jmath}
and
x
is not
\jmath
-equal to an element in the range of
f
. Then, there is a
g\in\eta
with

\forall u\,\bigl(z\in{\sf range}(g)\leftrightarrow(u\in{\sf range}(f)\vee u=y)\bigr)

and
\forall z\in{\sf dom}(f)\;f(z)=g(z)
.

By the usual argument, if
\imath
and
\jmath
are equal, then they are
fw
-elementary equivalent.
So,
{\sf INT}_{2{\tt a}}
is between
{\sf INT}_{2{\tt b}}
and
{\sf INT}_{1}
.

Isomorphism in
{\sf INT}_{2{\tt a}}
will be:
Ehrenfeucht-Fraïssé congruence
.

2.3.3.
Some basic Results

We start with two results about the category
{\sf INT}^{\sf fw}_{3}
. The first result will be useful in this paper.
The second will illustrate the power of sameness in the category.

Theorem 2.2
.

Let
\tau:\varXi\to\varTheta
and
\nu:\varTheta\to\varXi
be parameter-free translations. Let
\varPhi
be a
\varTheta
-sentence such that

\varPhi\vdash{\sf Th}_{\varXi}^{\tau}
and
\varPhi\vdash{\sf Th}_{\varTheta}^{\nu\tau}
and such that, for all
\varTheta
sentences
\varphi
,
we have
\varPhi\vdash\varphi^{\nu\tau}\leftrightarrow\varphi
. Consider any
\varTheta
-theory
U
that extends
\varPhi
. Let
V
be axiomatised by
the
\varXi
-sentences
\psi
such that
U\vdash\psi^{\tau}
. Then, there are interpretations
\imath:V\to U
based on
\tau
and

\jmath:U\to V
based on
\nu
, that witness that
U
is an
{\sf INT}^{\sf fw}_{3}
-retract of
V
.

Proof.

Since
U\vdash{\sf Th}_{\varXi}^{\tau}
, we indeed have an interpretation
\imath:V\to U
based on
\tau
. We have
V\vdash{\sf Th}_{\varTheta}^{\nu}
. Suppose

U\vdash\varphi
. Then,
U\vdash\varphi^{\nu\tau}
and, hence,
V\vdash\varphi^{\nu}
. So, we indeed have an interpretation
\jmath:V\to U
based on
\nu
.
Finally,
\varPhi
witnesses that
{\sf id}_{U}
and
\jmath\circ\imath
are
{\sf INT}^{\sf fw}_{3}
-equal.
❑

Theorem 2.3
.

Suppose
V
is an
{\sf INT}^{\sf fw}_{3}
-retract of a finitely axiomatised theory
A
. Then,
V
can be finitely axiomatised.

Proof.

Suppose
\imath:V\to A
and
\jmath:A\to V
. Let
\varPhi
witness the sameness of
{\sf ID}_{V}
and
\jmath\circ\imath
.
Then, we have:

\displaystyle V\vdash\varphi

\displaystyle\Rightarrow

\displaystyle A\vdash\varphi^{\imath}

\displaystyle\Rightarrow

\displaystyle A^{\jmath}\vdash\varphi^{\imath\jmath}

\displaystyle\Rightarrow

\displaystyle A^{\jmath}+\varPhi\vdash\varphi

\displaystyle\Rightarrow

\displaystyle V\vdash\varphi

❑

The following theorem tells us that if we have enough arithmetic, then interpretations can be reduced to a simpler form.

Theorem 2.4
.

Suppose
U
proves that its domain is infinite and

\imath:U\to{\sf PA}
. Then there is a one-dimensional, identity preserving interpretation

\jmath:U\to{\sf PA}
such that
\jmath
is verifiably, definably isomorphic to
\imath
.
Since in
PA
we have a definable tuple of parameters, we can restrict the parameters of
\jmath
to obtain
a one-dimensional, identity preserving, parameter-free interpretation
\jmath^{\ast}:U\to{\sf PA}
.

2.4.
Bounded Quantifiers

Here are some notational conventions and definitions. Let a signature
\varTheta
with designated binary relation symbol
\sqsubset
be given.

\bullet

We write
\mathchoice{\vbox{\halign{#\cr\kern-0.7pt\cr$\mkern 2.0mu\displaystyle\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}}\mkern-1.5mu\leaders{\hbox{$\displaystyle\mkern 0.0mu\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\montraita}$}}}\mkern 0.0mu$}}{\hfill}\mkern-1.5mu\fldr$\crcr\kern-0.3pt\cr$\hfil\displaystyle x\hfil$\crcr}}}{\vbox{\halign{#\cr\kern-0.7pt\cr$\mkern 2.0mu\textstyle\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}}\mkern-1.5mu\leaders{\hbox{$\textstyle\mkern 0.0mu\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\montraita}$}}}\mkern 0.0mu$}}{\hfill}\mkern-1.5mu\fldr$\crcr\kern-0.3pt\cr$\hfil\textstyle x\hfil$\crcr}}}{\vbox{\halign{#\cr\kern-0.7pt\cr$\mkern .

\bullet

A uniform quantifier block is bounded if it can be written as
\forall\mathchoice{\vbox{\halign{#\cr\kern-0.7pt\cr$\mkern 2.0mu\scriptscriptstyle\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}}\mkern-1.5mu\leaders{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\mkern
