0.0mu$}}{\hfill}\mkern-1.5mu\fldr$\crcr\kern-0.3pt\cr$\hfil\scriptscriptstyle x\hfil$\crcr}}}\,\varphi
.

\bullet

A formula is
\Delta_{0}
,
\Sigma_{0}
or
\Pi_{0}
iff all its quantifiers are bounded.
If
\varphi
is
\Sigma_{n}
, then the result of prefixing
\varphi
with a (possibly empty) block of bounded universal quantifiers
is
\Pi_{n+1}
. If
\psi
is
\Pi_{n}
, then the result of prefixing
\psi
with a (possibly empty) block of bounded existential quantifiers
is
\Sigma_{n+1}
.

\bullet

Let
U
be a theory in our language. A formula is
\Delta_{n}[U]
iff it is
U
-provably equivalent both to a
\Sigma_{n}
- and to a
\Pi_{n}
-formula.

\bullet

Let
\varGamma
be any formula-class of signature
\varTheta
. The class

\Delta_{0}(\varGamma)
is the result of closing off
\varGamma
under the propositional connectives and bounded quantification.

\bullet

Let
\tau
be a translation from
\varXi
to
\varTheta
. Let
\varGamma
be any formula class. We say that
\tau
is a
\varGamma
-translation iff

\alpha_{\tau}
,
\delta_{\tau}
and each of the
P_{\tau}
are in
\varGamma
.

2.5.
Dorroh-Rings

Given a commutative ring
\mathcal{R}
, We say that
\mathcal{R}
is a
Dorroh-ring
, if there is an ideal
J
of
\mathcal{R}
such that

\mathcal{R}/J\simeq\mathbb{Z}
. Such an ideal
J
is said to
witness
that
\mathcal{R}
is a Dorroh ring.
The polynomial ring
\mathbb{Z}[{\sf X}]
is an example of a Dorroh ring, as witnessed by the ideal
J
consisting of
p({\sf X})\in\mathbb{Z}[{\sf X}]
such that
p(0)=0
.

Remark 2.5
.

A
non-unital ring
, aka
rng
, is a ring that has no identity element. In Theorem 1 of
[
Dor32
]
, Joe Lee Dorroh gives a construction that adjoins an identity element to a non-unital ring; the proof makes it clear that the range of the Dorroh extension construction applied to all non-unital rings precisely consists, modulo isomorphism,
of the Dorroh rings. This is the reason, we opted for the name
Dorroh ring
.

Lemma 2.6
.

Suppose
J
is an ideal of the ring
\mathcal{R}
. Then,
J
witnesses that
\mathcal{R}
is a Dorroh ring iff, for every element
a
of
\mathcal{R}
, there is
a unique pair
j\in J
and
z\in\mathbb{Z}
such that
a=j+z
.

Proof.

Suppose
\mathcal{R}
is a Dorroh ring. By induction, we show that
that the witnessing isomorphism maps
{\underline{n}}:=\overbrace{1+\ldots+1}^{n\mathrm{-times}}
to
n
. It follows that
every
J
- equivalence class contains either
{\underline{n}}
or
-{\underline{n}}
. Moreover,
{\underline{m}}\neq 0
, for
m\neq 0
in

\mathcal{R}/J
, and,
a fortiori
, in
\mathcal{R}
. We identify the
{\underline{n}}
and
-{\underline{n}}
in
\mathcal{R}
with
\mathbb{Z}
.
It follows that every
a
in
\mathcal{R}
can be written as
j+z
, for some
j\in J
and
z\in\mathbb{Z}
.
Suppose
j+z=j^{\prime}+z^{\prime}
, for
j,j^{\prime}\in J
and
z,z^{\prime}\in\mathbb{Z}
. It follows that
j-j^{\prime}=z^{\prime}-z
.
So,
z=z^{\prime}
in
\mathcal{R}/J
, and hence in
\mathcal{R}
. So, also
j=j^{\prime}
.

Conversely, suppose that, for every element of
a
of
\mathcal{R}
, there is
a unique pair
j\in J
and
z\in\mathbb{Z}
such that
a=j+z
.
Ergo, there will be an element of
z
in each equivalence class.
There cannot be two integers in one equivalence class by uniqueness.
❑

Lemma 2.7
.

The witnessing ideal of a Dorroh ring is unique.

Proof.

Let
\pi
,
\pi^{\prime}
be the projection functions, respectively, from
\mathcal{R}
to
\mathcal{R}/J
and
from
\mathcal{R}
to
\mathcal{R}/J^{\prime}
and let
f
and
f^{\prime}
be the witnessing isomorphisms
from
\mathcal{R}/J
to
\mathbb{Z}
and from
\mathcal{R}/J^{\prime}
to
\mathbb{Z}
.
We have seen that
\pi\circ f
and
\pi^{\prime}\circ f^{\prime}
coincide on
\mathbb{Z}

as part of
\mathcal{R}
. It follows that any
a
is uniquely of the form

j+z
and
j^{\prime}+z
. So,
j=j^{\prime}
and, thus,
J=J^{\prime}
.
❑

In our context, an ordered commutative ring
\mathcal{R}
comes equipped with a
strict
total order
<
that satisfies: (1) if
x<y
then
x+z<y+z
; and (2) if
x<y
and
z>0
, then
xz<yz
. Under this definition, an ordered ring is easily seen to be an integral domain.
6
6

6

Surprisingly, if (1) and (2) are reformulated by replacing
<
with
\leqslant
, then the resulting definition no longer guarantees that the ring is an integral domain. One such example is the ring
\mathcal{R}=\mathbb{R}[{\sf X}]/J
, where
J
is the ideal generated by
{\sf X}^{2}
, and the ordering is defined by
a+b{\sf X}\leqslant c+d{\sf X}
iff
a<c
, or
a=c
and
b\leqslant d
. Note that
{\sf X}^{2}=0
, so
\mathcal{R}
is not an integral domain.

An
ordered Dorroh ring
is an ordered commutative ring with the extra stipulation that, if
j>0
, then
z<j
, for

z\in\mathbb{Z}
and
j\in J
. We immediately see:

Lemma 2.8
.

An ordered Dorroh ring is discrete.

We write
\mathcal{R}^{{\geqslant 0}}
, for the non-negative part of an ordered ring
\mathcal{R}
.
The following theorem plays a key role in the proofs of our main results:

Theorem 2.9
.

Suppose
\mathcal{R}
is an ordered Dorroh ring. Then,
the natural numbers are
\Delta_{0}
-definable in
\mathcal{R}^{{\geqslant 0}}
.
It follows that the integers are definable in
\mathcal{R}
.

Proof.

We work in
\mathcal{R}^{{\geqslant 0}}
. We define:

\bullet

x\in\omega_{{\mathcal{R}}}
iff

\forall y\mathbin{\leqslant}x\;\exists u_{0}\mathbin{\leqslant}y\;\exists u_{1}\mathbin{\leqslant}y\;\exists u_{2}\mathbin{\leqslant}y\,\exists u_{3}\mathbin{\leqslant}y\;\;y=u_{0}^{2}+u_{1}^{2}+u_{2}^{2}+u_{3}^{2}
.

Clearly, every natural number
n
in
\mathcal{R}^{{\geqslant 0}}
does satisfy the condition, by
Lagrange’s Four Squares Theorem.

Consider any
a
in
\mathcal{R}^{{\geqslant 0}}
. The element
a
can be written as
j+z
, where
j\in J
and
z\in\mathbb{Z}
.
So,
a^{2}=j^{2}+2zj+z^{2}
. Since,
(j^{2}+2zj)\in J
, we find that
a^{2}
is of the form
j^{\prime}+z^{\prime}
, where
z^{\prime}\geqslant 0
.
It follows that, no element
j^{\ast}+z^{\ast}
, for
z^{\ast}<0
, can be a sum of squares. Consider any element
b=j^{\star}+z^{\star}
,
for
0\neq j^{\star}\in J
. Clearly,

b-|z^{\star}|-1<b
and
b-|z^{\star}|-1
is not a sum of squares. So,
b
is not in
\omega_{{\mathcal{R}}}
.
❑

If we call a number
four-squarable
if it is the sum of four squares, then
\omega_{{\mathcal{R}}}
consists of the

hereditarily four-squarable
numbers of
\mathcal{R}
.
7
7

7

If
P
is any property of numbers,
x
is
hereditarily
P
if

\forall y\mathbin{\leqslant}x\,P(y)
and
x
is
essentially
P
if

\forall y\mathop{\geqslant}x\,P(y)
.

Remark 2.10
.

Clearly, the only Dorroh ring of which the non-negative part satisfies Lagrange’s Four Squares Theorem is

\mathbb{Z}
.

Remark 2.11
.

The definability of
\omega
in a Dorroh ring allows us to unravel recursive definitions. In this way we can obtain
\Sigma_{1}
explicit definitions of functions defined by recursion such as the exponentiation
(
with natural exponents
)
, generalized sum
(
of true finite length
)
et al.

2.6.
Real Closure

There are several equivalent formulations of the notion of a real closed field. For definiteness, we say that a real closed field is an ordered field
\mathcal{K}
in which (1) every non-negative element has a square root, and every odd-degree polynomial in
\mathcal{K}[{\sf X}]
has a root in
\mathcal{K}
. Given ordered fields
\mathcal{K}
and
\overline{\mathcal{K}}
,
we say that
\overline{\mathcal{K}}
is
a real closure of

\mathcal{K}
if (1)
\overline{\mathcal{K}}
extends
\mathcal{K}

as an ordered field, (2) every element of
\overline{\mathcal{K}}
is algebraic over
\mathcal{K}
, and
(3)
\overline{\mathcal{K}}
is real closed. By a classical theorem of Emil Artin and Otto Schreier, every ordered field has a real closure; moreover we have the following uniqueness feature of real closures:

Theorem 2.12
.

[
Lan02
, Theorem 2.9, Chapter XI]
Any two real closures of an ordered field
\mathcal{K}
are isomorphic over
\mathcal{K}
.

Thus, every ordered field
\mathcal{K}
has a unique real closure (up to isomorphism), which we will denote by
{\sf rc}(\mathcal{K})
.

Given an ordered commutative ring
\mathcal{R}
, we write
{\sf rc}(\mathcal{R})
for
{\sf rc}(\mathsf{fc}(\mathcal{R}))
,
where
\mathsf{fc}(\mathcal{R})
is the field of quotients of
\mathcal{R}
.

Given certain ordered commutative rings
\mathcal{R}
that are interpretable in the standard model
\mathbb{N}
of arithmetic, we will need (in Sections
5
and
7
) to interpret
\mathsf{rc}(\mathcal{R})
in
\mathbb{N}
. Among the many ways of accomplishing this, we find James Madison’s representation of
{\sf rc}(\mathcal{R})
in

[
Mad70
]
) to be the most convenient for our purposes:
we start with all elements of the form
(f,n)
, where
f\in\mathcal{R}[{\sf X}]

(here
f
is represented as a finite sequence of elements of
\mathcal{R}
) and
n
is a natural number, such that

{\sf rc}(\mathcal{R})\models\underbrace{``\textnormal{There exists at least $n$ different roots of $f$}"}_{(*)}.

We observe that the above property can readily be verified already in
\mathcal{R}
: by quantifier elimination, for each
(f,n)

as above there is a quantifier-free formula
\psi(\bar{x})
such that

{\sf rc}(\mathcal{R})\models{(*)}\leftrightarrow\psi(\bar{b})
,
where
\bar{b}\in\mathcal{R}
are all the coefficients of
f
. Since
\psi(\bar{b})
is quantifier-free and
\bar{b}\in\mathcal{R}
, the sentence
\psi(\bar{b})

is absolute between
{\sf rc}(\mathcal{R})
and
\mathcal{R}
. Let us call a pair
(f,n)
satisfying
(*)
an
\mathcal{R}
-notation.
On the set of
\mathcal{R}
-notations, we consider the equivalence relation
\sim_{\mathcal{R}}
defined as follows

(f,n)\sim_{\mathcal{R}}(g,k)\;:\Leftrightarrow\;{\sf rc}(\mathcal{R})\models``\textnormal{The $n$-th root of $f$ is equal to the $k$-th root of $g$}".

Once again, whether
(f,n)\sim_{\mathcal{R}}(g,k)
holds depends only on the atomic diagram of
\mathcal{R}
.
Similarly, one can define addition and multiplication on the set of equivalence classes of
\sim_{\mathcal{R}}
.
We define the inclusion map
\iota_{\mathcal{R}}:{\mathcal{R}}\rightarrow{\sf rc}(\mathcal{R})
, called
the standard embedding
, to be the mapping

\iota_{\mathcal{R}}(r):=[({\langle 1,-r\rangle},1)]_{\sim_{\mathcal{R}}}.

We will follow the usual practice of identifying the elements from
\mathcal{R}

with their images under
\iota_{\mathcal{R}}
.
8
8

8

This
is justified by an elementary fact of model theory that states that given an embedding

e:\mathcal{M}\hookrightarrow\mathcal{N}
of a structure
\mathcal{M}
as a submodel of a
structure
\mathcal{N}
, there is a structure
\mathcal{N}^{*}
such that
\mathcal{M}
is a

submodel
of
\mathcal{N}^{*}
, and there is an isomorphism

f:\mathcal{N}^{*}\rightarrow\mathcal{N}
such that
f\circ i=e
, where

i:\mathcal{M}\hookrightarrow\mathcal{N}^{*}
is the inclusion embedding

In the following, we will use the standard notation for some elements of the
real closure of
\mathcal{R}
and omit the brackets denoting the equivalence class. For example, instead of writing

[{\langle 1,0,-r\rangle},2]_{\sim_{\mathcal{R}}}
we will be writing
\sqrt{r}
.

We observe that if
\mathcal{R}\subseteq\mathcal{R}^{\prime}
are both ordered commutative rings, then thanks to elimination of quantifiers in real closed fields,
{\sf rc}(\mathcal{R})
is an elementary submodel of
{\sf rc}(\mathcal{R}^{\prime})
. In particular,
for each polynomial
f
with coefficients in
R
, and for each
n\in\mathbb{N}
,
we find that
(f,n)
is an
\mathcal{R}
-notation iff
(f,n)
is an
\mathcal{R}^{\prime}
-notation.
Similarly, if
(f,m)
and
(g,n)
are two
\mathcal{R}
-notations,
then
(f,m)\sim_{\mathcal{R}}(g,n)
iff
(f,m)\sim_{\mathsf{\mathcal{R}^{\prime}}}(g,n)
.

The above construction can be executed in the case of the ordered field of rational numbers
\mathbb{Q}
and the presentation should make it clear that both the construction of
\mathbb{Q}
and
{\sf rc}(\mathbb{Q})
can be done in
\mathbb{N}
(in fact the presentations of both fields are decidable).

Remark 2.13
.

The literature gives several ways to construct the real algebraic numbers in
\mathbb{N}
. What is more, they provide interpretations
of the theory
RCF
of real closed fields in Robinson’s arithmetic
Q
. See the papers
[
FF02
]
,
[
FF13
]
by Fernando Ferreira and Gilda Ferreira, the thesis
[
Dmi19
]
(available online) by Anna Dmitrieva, and the following FOM posts by Harvey Friedman:

https://cs.nyu.edu/pipermail/fom/1999-August/003345.html

https://cs.nyu.edu/pipermail/fom/1999-December/003523.html

3.
The Framework

In this section, we will build the foundation on which the results of our paper are built.
We will treat the case of one-dimensional parameter-free interpretations and morphisms between
interpretations with one parameter.

3.1.
Interpretations that live below a Bound

Consider a signature
\varTheta
with a designated binary predicate symbol
\sqsubset
and an arbitrary signature
\varXi
.
Let
\tau
be a parameter-free one-dimensional translation from
\varXi
to
\varTheta
and
let
\theta(x)
be a
\varTheta
-formula with one free variable.
9
9

9

Even though in this paper
\sqsubset
can be safely replaced with
\leqslant
(or with
<
), we have opted for this more general approach to bounded quantifiers because the sequel of this paper uses
\in
for bounding purposes.

We compute
\varphi^{\tau,[x]}
as follows: we follow the clauses of the inductive definition
of
\varphi^{\tau}
in the usual way, only we take:

\bullet

(\forall y\,\psi)^{\tau,[x]}:=\forall y\mathop{\sqsubset}x\,(\delta_{\tau}(y)\to\psi^{\tau,[x]})
,

\bullet

(\exists y\,\psi)^{\tau,[x]}:=\exists y\mathop{\sqsubset}x\,(\delta_{\tau}(y)\wedge\psi^{\tau,[x]})
.

We define
{\mathfrak{U}}(\tau,\theta)
as the theory axiomatised by:

A.

{\sf Th}_{\varXi}^{\tau}
,

B.

\exists x\,x\in\theta\wedge\forall y\mathbin{\in}\theta\,\forall z\mathbin{\in}\delta_{\tau}\;z\sqsubset y
.

Clause (A) guarantees that
\tau
carries an interpretation of Predicate Logic of signature
\varXi
in
{\mathfrak{U}}(\tau,\theta)
.
We have the following insight.

We say that a translation
\tau
is a
\varGamma
-translation iff
\delta_{\tau}
and the
{\sf P}_{\tau}

are all in
\varGamma
.

Lemma 3.1
.

Suppose
\tau
is a
\varGamma
-translation and
\theta
is a
\varGamma
-formula.
Let
{\mathfrak{U}}:={\mathfrak{U}}(\tau,\theta)
.
Then
\varphi^{\tau}
is
\Delta_{1}[{\mathfrak{U}}](\varGamma)
, for any
\varXi
-formula
\varphi
.

Proof.

We assume the conditions of the theorem. So,

{\mathfrak{U}}+\theta(x)\vdash\varphi^{\tau}\leftrightarrow\varphi^{\tau,[x]}
. Moreover,

\varphi^{\tau,[x]}
is
\Delta_{0}(\varGamma)
.
So, we have, using that
\theta
is
{\mathfrak{T}}
-provably non-empty:

\displaystyle{\mathfrak{U}}\vdash\exists x\mathbin{\in}\theta\;\varphi^{\tau,[x]}

\displaystyle\to

\displaystyle\varphi^{\tau}

\displaystyle\to

\displaystyle\forall x\mathbin{\in}\theta\,\varphi^{\tau,[x]}

\displaystyle\to

\displaystyle\exists x\mathbin{\in}\theta\;\varphi^{\tau,[x]}

It follows that
\varphi^{\tau}
is, modulo
{\mathfrak{U}}
-provable equivalence, both in
\Sigma_{1}(\varGamma)
and in
\Pi_{1}(\varGamma)
.
❑

Remark 3.2
.

Let
\text{{\straighttheta}}_{\tau}(x)
be the formula
\forall y\in\delta_{\tau}\;y\sqsubset x
.
Then,
{\mathfrak{U}}(\tau,\theta)
extends
{\mathfrak{U}}(\tau,\text{{\straighttheta}}_{\tau})
.
We work with a
\theta
that possibly differs from
\text{{\straighttheta}}_{\tau}
, since such a
\theta
is conceivably less complex than
\text{{\straighttheta}}_{\tau}
.

3.2.
Bounds and Retracts

We have the following theorem.

Theorem 3.3
.

Let
\varGamma
be a set of
\varTheta
-formulas. Suppose that
\tau:\varXi\to\varTheta
is a
\varGamma
-translation and
\nu:\varTheta\to\varXi
.
Suppose
\varPsi
is a
\varTheta
-sentence such that
\varPsi\vdash{\sf Th}_{\varXi}^{\tau}
and
\varPsi\vdash{\sf Th}_{\varTheta}^{\nu\tau}
and

\varPsi\vdash\varphi\leftrightarrow\varphi^{\nu\tau}
, for all
\varTheta
-sentences
\varphi
.
Let
\theta(x)
be in
\varGamma
. Let
\varPhi:=\varPsi\wedge\exists x\,x\in\theta\wedge\forall y\mathbin{\in}\theta\,\forall z\mathbin{\in}\delta_{\tau}\;z\sqsubset y
.
Then, all
\varTheta
-sentences are
\Delta_{1}[\varPhi](\varGamma)
.

Proof.

We assume the conditions of the theorem. By Lemma
3.1
, the sentence

\varphi^{\nu\tau}
is
\Delta_{1}[\varPhi](\varGamma)
. By the
\varPhi
-provable equivalence of

\varphi
and
\varphi^{\nu\tau}
, we find that
\varphi
is also in
\Delta_{1}[\varPhi](\varGamma)
.
❑

We give the corollary that we will use in our arguments.

Corollary 3.4
.

Let
\mathcal{M}
be a
\varTheta
-structure, where
\varTheta
has a designated binary relation
\sqsubset
,
and let
\mathcal{N}
be a structure in any signature.
Suppose we have one-dimensional parameter-free interpretations
\imath:\mathcal{N}\to\mathcal{M}
and

\jmath:\mathcal{M}\to\mathcal{N}
that witness that
\mathcal{M}
is an
{\sf INT}_{3}^{\sf fw}
-retract of
\mathcal{N}
.
Let
\tau
and
\theta
be in some prescribed formula class
\varGamma
.
Suppose that

\mathcal{M}\models\exists x\,\theta(x)\wedge\forall x\mathbin{\in}\theta\,\forall y\mathbin{\in}\delta_{\imath}\;x\sqsubset y
.

Then, there are a
\varTheta
-sentence
\varPhi
, and a set
of
\Delta_{1}[\varPhi](\varGamma)
-formulas
\mathcal{X}
, such
that
\varPhi+\mathcal{X}
axiomatises
\mathrm{Th}(\mathcal{M})
.

So, specifically, if a theory
U
has a model
\mathcal{M}
satisfying the conditions of Corollary
3.4
.
then, there is a completion of
U
that is of the specified complexity.

3.3.
How to witness an
{\sf INT}_{3}^{\sf fw}
-Retract

In Sections
4
and
5
we employ our first way to witness that a pair
\tau
,
\nu
supports an

{\sf INT}_{3}^{\sf fw}
-retract by constructing an
{\sf INT}_{1}
-retract.
In the paper, we will consider a definable isomorphism
\digamma_{p}(x,y)
with parameter
p

between
\tau\circ\nu
and the identity translation on
\varTheta
. The sentence
\varPsi
will
state that
\digamma_{p}
is an isomorphism as desired, for some
p
.

In Sections
6
and
7
we employ our second way, in which we construct an
{\sf INT}_{2{\tt a}}
-retract.
Here the sentence
\varPsi
consists of the atomic clauses and the
back-and-forth clauses of the Ehrenfeucht-Fraïssé game between
\tau\circ\nu
and the identity translation on
\varTheta
.

Open Question 3.5
.

Is there yet another way to construct a witnessing sentence for an
{\sf INT}_{3}^{\sf fw}
-Retract?
❍

4.
A Restricted Completion of
{\sf PA}^{-}

Our basic argument for the simplest case works as follows. We produce a model
\mathbb{M}
of
{\sf PA}^{-}
, in which
we can define the standard cut
\omega
and specify a non-empty set of elements above
\omega
.
We build an internal copy of
\mathbb{M}
inside
\omega
. Using sequentiality, we construct
a definable isomorphism between the internal copy and the original model. This makes the framework of
Section
3
applicable.

4.1.
The Second Standard Model

We use the terminology
the second standard model
(
of
{\sf PA}^{-}
)

to refer to the model
\mathbb{M}:=\mathbb{Z}[{\sf X}]^{{\geqslant 0}}
, i.e.,
the non-negative part of
\mathbb{Z}[{\sf X}]
considered as an ordered ring.
Specifically,
\mathbb{M}
consists of
the polynomials
p({\sf X})=a_{n}{\sf X}^{n}+a_{n-1}{\sf X}^{n-1}+\cdots+a_{0}
, with integer
coefficients, where
a_{n}\geqslant 0
and (
a_{n}\neq 0
, if
n\neq 0
), with the obvious operations.
The ordering is determined by stipulating that
{\sf X}>\omega
. More formally, we first deem
p({\sf X})>0
if
a_{n}>0
, and then we define the full ordering by:
p_{1}({\sf X})>p_{2}({\sf X})
iff
p_{1}({\sf X})-p_{2}({\sf X})>0.

We identify the standard numbers
\omega
in the usual way
with constant polynomials.

4.2.
\Delta_{0}
-definitions of Salient Classes

Since
\mathbb{M}
is the non-negative part of the Dorroh ring
\mathbb{Z}[{\sf X}]
, we can give a
\Delta_{0}
-definition of the natural numbers
\omega_{{\mathbb{M}}}

using the four squares theorem. In other words, these will be the hereditarily four-squarable numbers.
This definition is just one of the many ways to define the naturals by a
\Delta_{0}
-formula. Here is an alternative way of doing it.
We define the natural numbers as the hereditarily odd-or-even numbers. Formally:

\bullet

The set of numbers that are odd or even is defined as follows:

x\in{\sf odev}:=\exists y\mathbin{\leqslant}x\,(x=2y\vee x=2y+1)
.

\bullet

We now can define
\omega_{{\mathbb{M}}}
by:

x\in\omega_{{\mathbb{M}}}:=\forall y\mathbin{\leqslant}x\;x\in{\sf odev}
.
The elements of
\omega_{{\mathbb{M}}}
are the
hereditarily odd-or-even numbers
.

We define sequences in our model
\mathbb{M}
using the
\Delta_{0}
-defined
\omega_{{\mathbb{M}}}

as discussed in Section
2.2
.

4.3.
Interpreting
\mathbb{N}
in
\mathbb{M}
.

We simply do this by relativising the quantifiers to
\omega_{{\mathbb{M}}}
. Let us call this interpretation
N
.

4.4.
Interpreting
\mathbb{M}
in
\mathbb{N}
.

We can build a copy of
\mathbb{M}
inside
\mathbb{N}
in any number of ways, seeing that
we have the full power of
\mathbb{N}
at our disposal. Our development will not depend on any specific
feature of how we do it.

Here is one sketch of one way to do it. We first construct an internal model of
\mathbb{Z}

inside
\mathbb{N}
. This can be done in many ways. For specificity, we use the following one.
We employ
{\langle 0,n\rangle}
for
+n
and
{\langle 1,m+1\rangle}
for
-(m+1)
. We note that this interpretation is identity preserving.

We describe a polynomial
a_{n}{\sf X}^{n}+\dots+a_{0}
as a sequence
(z_{n},\dots,z_{0})
, where
the
z_{i}
are the representatives in our copy of
\mathbb{Z}
of the integers corresponding to the
a_{i}
.
We demand
a_{n}\geqslant 0
, and
a_{n}>0
, if
n\neq 0
.
We can use the sequences given by the
\beta
-function or, alternatively, the
the sequences can defined by iterated pairing or using any other trick to construct sequences.
However, we will employ the same notations for the projection- and length-functions
as we used in the
\beta
-case.

Defining addition and multiplication for these sequences corresponding to addition and multiplication of polynomials is clearly within the resources
of
\mathbb{N}
. This gives us an
\mathbb{N}
-internal model
\mathbb{M}^{\ast}
that is
isomorphic to
\mathbb{M}
.
Let the interpretation of
\mathbb{M}^{\ast}
in
\mathbb{N}
be
M
.
By Theorem
2.4
, we can make our interpretation identity-preserving, but nothing in what we are doing depends on that.

4.5.
The Application Function

We define the function
app
.
This function takes as input a representation
a
of a polynomial and an element
x
and gives as output the
value of the polynomial applied to
x
.

\bullet

x\oplus{\langle i,n\rangle}:=x+n
, if
i=0
and
x\oplus{\langle i,n\rangle}:=x-n
, if
i=1
and
0<n\leqslant x
and

x\oplus{\langle i,n\rangle}=0
, otherwise. We note that minus is definable here by the subtraction axiom of

{\sf PA}^{-}
.

\bullet

{\sf app}(a,x,b)
iff, there is a sequence
s
of the same length as
a
, such that
\pi(s,0)=\pi(a,0)
and,
for all
i
such that
i+1<{\sf length}(a)
,

\pi(s,i+1)=(\pi(s,i)\cdot x)\oplus\pi(a,i+1)
, and

\pi(s,{\sf length}(a)-1)=b
.

It is easy to see that
app
indeed defines application as long as
x
is so large that
the third clause of the definition of
\oplus
is never active in the computation of the value.
This will certainly be the case if
x\not\in\omega_{{\mathbb{M}}}
and this is the case we are interested in.

We note that
{\sf app}(\overbrace{{\langle 1_{{\mathbb{Z}}},0_{{\mathbb{Z}}},\dots,0_{{\mathbb{Z}}}\rangle}}^{\text{length }n+1},x)=x^{n}
. Since the function

n\mapsto\overbrace{{\langle 1_{{\mathbb{Z}}},0_{{\mathbb{Z}}},\dots,0_{{\mathbb{Z}}}\rangle}}^{\text{length }n+1}
is clearly arithmetically definable,
the function
n\mapsto x^{n}
is
\mathbb{M}
-definable.

4.6.
Completing
{\sf PA}^{-}

We apply Corollary
3.4
with:

\bullet

\mathbb{M}
in the role of
\mathcal{M}
,

\bullet

\leqslant
in the role of
\sqsubset
,
10
10

10

Here we can equally well use
<
instead of
\leqslant
, since we are interested in bounding the standard numbers by a non-standard one.

\bullet

\mathbb{N}
in the role of

\mathcal{N}
,

\bullet

N
in the role of
\imath
,

\bullet

M
in the role of
\jmath
.

\bullet

\omega_{{\mathbb{M}}}^{\sf c}
, the complement of
\omega_{{\mathbb{M}}}
in
\mathbb{M}
, in the role of
\theta
.

\bullet

Let
{\mathfrak{F}}_{x}(a):={\sf app}(a,x)
. Then,
{\mathfrak{F}}_{{\sf X}}(a)
is an isomorphism between

\mathbb{M}^{\ast}
and
\mathbb{M}
. The statement that
{\mathfrak{F}}_{x}
is an isomorphism
for some
x
delivers our desired witness of an
{\sf INT}^{\sf fw}_{3}
-retract.

It is easy to see that our choices realise the conditions of the Corollary.
We note that
\tau_{\sf N}
and
\omega_{{\mathbb{M}}}^{\sf c}
are
\Delta_{0}
.
So it follows that we have a sentence
\varPhi
, and a set
\mathcal{X}

of
\Delta_{1}
-formulas
X
(over
\varPhi
), such
that
\varPhi+\mathcal{X}
axiomatises
\mathsf{Th}(\mathbb{M})
.

We conclude:

Theorem 4.1
.

The theory
{\sf PA}^{-}
has a completion that is axiomatised by a sentence
\varPhi
plus a set of
\Sigma_{1}
-sentences.

A nice consequence of our development is:

Theorem 4.2
.

In
\mathbb{Z}[{\sf X}]
, we can define composition
P({\sf X})\circ Q({\sf X}):=P(Q({\sf X}))
in parameter
{\sf X}
.

Proof.

We consider a one-dimensional identity-preserving version of
M
. Then
{\mathfrak{F}}_{{\sf X}}
is a bijection.
We take
P({\sf X})\circ Q({\sf X}):={\sf app}({\mathfrak{F}}^{-1}_{{\sf X}}(P({\sf X})),Q({\sf X}))
.
❑

4.7.
Free Riders

We stated Theorem
4.1
for
{\sf PA}^{-}
, but
{\sf Th}(\mathbb{M})

satisfies many arithmetically true extensions of
{\sf PA}^{-}
, for example, any
arithmetically true sentence relativised to
N
. These are the free riders
of the argument.

However, many of such examples are somewhat contrived. Here is an example that is not
contrived. Since
\mathbb{Z}
is a unique factorisation domain, so is
\mathbb{Z}[{\sf X}]
.
Clearly, the units in this case are
+1
and
-1
. It follows that the elements of

\mathbb{M}
have unique factorisation with unit
1
. Using the existence of sequences,
as given by
{\sf seq}^{\ast}
,
we can define in
\mathbb{M}
a partial function
\Pi
that computes the product of the
elements of an
\ast
-sequence.
We define:

\bullet

{\sf pseq}^{\ast}
iff

s
is a
\ast
-sequence and the elements
of
s
are all prime and, for all
i<j<{\sf length}(s)
, we have
\pi(s,i)\leqslant\pi(s,j)
.

\bullet

s\approx s^{\prime}:={\sf length}(s)={\sf length}(s^{\prime})\wedge\forall i\mathbin{<}{\sf length}(s)\;\pi(s,i)=\pi^{\prime}(s^{\prime},i)
.

\bullet

{\sf UF}:=\forall x\,\exists s\mathbin{\in}{\sf pseq}^{\ast}\,\forall s^{\prime}\mathbin{\in}{\sf pseq}^{\ast}\;(\Pi(s^{\prime})=x\leftrightarrow s\approx s^{\prime})
.

Then we have:

Theorem 4.3
.

The theory
{\sf PA}^{-}+{\sf UF}
has a completion that is axiomatised by a sentence
\varPhi
plus a set of
\Sigma_{1}
-sentences.
Specifically, this completion can be taken to be
{\sf Th}(\mathbb{M})
.

Of course, there are all kinds of consequences of unique factorisation that are easier to formulate. An example is the existence
of greatest common divisors.

Remark 4.4
.

We note that we do
not
have the Euclidean Division Axiom internally in our model: think of dividing
{\sf X}
by 2. We can modify our model
by allowing the coefficients
a_{i}
for
i\neq 0
to be in
\mathbb{Q}
. Thus, we obtain the model

({\sf X}\cdot\mathbb{Q}[{\sf X}]+\mathbb{Z})^{{\geqslant 0}}
. We can repeat our development almost verbatim
for this model. It satisfies the Euclidean Division Axiom internally.
We note that the associated discretely ordered commutative ring is
not
a
Euclidean Domain. If it were, it would also be a Factorisation Domain, but clearly
{\sf X}
does not have a factorisation
in irreducible elements.
However, the ring is a Bézout domain, so we still have greatest common divisors.
See:

https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bézout_domain

4.8.
Definitional Equivalence

We have found an interpretation
N
of (an isomorphic copy of)
\mathbb{N}
in
\mathbb{M}
and an interpretation
M
of
(an isomorphic copy of)
\mathbb{M}
in
\mathbb{N}
. These two interpretations form a bi-interpretation as witnessed by
{\mathfrak{F}}_{\sf X}

and a suitable parameter-free
{\mathfrak{G}}
. Clearly,
N
is one-dimensional and identity-preserving.
Theorem
2.4
tells us that we can
always assume that
M
is one-dimensional and identity-preserving. If we expand the signature of the
language of
\mathbb{M}
with
a constant for
X
, the witnessing isomorphism
{\mathfrak{F}}_{\sf X}
becomes parameter-free.
We may now apply the main theorem of
[
FV25
]
, and conclude that
\mathbb{N}
and
the expansion
(\mathbb{M},{\sf X})
of
\mathbb{M}
are definitionally equivalent.

5.
A restricted Completion of
IOpen

Recall that
IOpen
is the theory in the arithmetical signature that is the result of strengthening
{\sf PA}^{-}
with all
instants of the induction scheme
for quantifier-free formulae.
We redirect the reader to
[
HP93
, Chapter I]
for information on how much arithmetic can be developed in
IOpen
.

5.1.
Shepherdson’s Model

In this subsection we analyze the recursive nonstandard model of
{\sf IOpen}
constructed by John Shepherdson in his pioneering paper
[
She64
]
; we denote this model with
\mathbb{S}
. The elements of
\mathbb{S}
are
Puiseux polynomials
over the field
{\sf rc}(\mathbb{Q})
of real algebraic numbers. More specifically, the elements of
\mathbb{S}
are formal expressions of the form
11
11

11

Shepherdson interchanges the positive and negative
powers of Puiseux series in which
{\sf X}
is deemed to be an infinitesimal (and thus
{\sf X}^{-1}
becomes infinite). We will follow his lead here.
:

p({\sf X})=a_{n}{\sf X}^{n/q}+a_{n-1}{\sf X}^{(n-1)/q}+\ldots+a_{1}{\sf X}^{1/q}+a_{0},

where each
a_{i}\in{\sf rc}(\mathbb{Q})
,
a_{0}\in\mathbb{Z}
,
n\in\mathbb{N}
, and
q\in\mathbb{N}\setminus\{0\}
; furthermore
a_{n}>0
if
n\neq 0
; and
a_{n}\geqslant 0
if
n=0
. As usual, we say that
a_{n}
is the leading coefficient of
p
as above.
Addition and multiplication in
\mathbb{S}
are given by the usual addition and multiplication operations on polynomials, and the ordering is given in such a way as to make
{\sf X}
infinite. More specificially, for
p,q\in\mathbb{S}
,
p>q
iff the leading coefficient of
p-q
is positive.

The above presentation of elements of
\mathbb{S}
is non-unique, because some of the
a_{i}
’s might equal
0
. By omitting the
0
terms,
we obtain the notion of a
standard form
, which is an expression of the form

a_{n}{\sf X}^{q_{n}}+a_{n-1}{\sf X}^{q_{n-1}}+\ldots+a_{1}{\sf X}^{q_{1}}+a_{0},

where

1
.

each
a_{i}\in{\sf rc}(\mathbb{Q})
, and
a_{i}\neq 0
if
i\neq 0
,

2
.

a_{n}\geqslant 0
,

3
.

a_{0}\in\mathbb{Z}
,

4
.

each
q_{i}\in\mathbb{Q}^{{>0}}
, and
q_{i}>q_{j}
for
i>j
.

5.2.
The Natural Numbers in
\mathbb{S}

Since
\mathbb{S}
is the non-negative part of a Dorroh ring (as witnessed by the ideal consisting of
p({\sf X})\in\mathbb{S}

such that
p(0)=0
), we can define the natural numbers using the four squares theorem.
There are, of course, other possibilities that we briefly discus below.

Since the notions “prime” and “irreducible” coincide in
\mathbb{Z}
, the following proposition implies that the irreducible elements of
\mathbb{S}
are precisely the prime numbers in
\mathbb{N}
:

Proposition 5.1
.

If
a
is irreducible in
\mathbb{S}
, then
a\in\mathbb{N}
.

This result is well-known from the literature. For completeness, we provide a proof in Appendix
A
.
We can now alternatively define:

\bullet

\omega_{{\mathbb{S}}}(x):=x=0\vee\exists y\mathbin{\leqslant}2x\,\bigl(x\leqslant y\wedge{\sf irred}(y)\bigr)
.

The above definition works by Proposition
5.1
in combination with
Bertrand’s postulate
: for
every natural number
n>0
there is a prime number between
n
and
2n
.
We note that Bertrand’s postulate is a classical theorem of Number Theory (which was established by Chebyshev).

5.3.
Representing the real algebraic Numbers in
\mathbb{S}

Locally, we will use
\alpha,\beta,\dots
elements of
{\sf rc}(\mathbb{Q})
, i.e., for real algebraic numbers.

We can take over the definition of
x^{n}
(where
x
ranges over
\mathbb{S}
), for
n\in\omega
from Section
4.5
.
It follows that we can also define
x^{n/m}
for
m\in\omega
as the maximal solution
y
, if any, of
x^{n}=y^{m}
.
We note that this function does not need to be total.

We remind the reader that we constructed codes of real algebraic numbers in
\mathbb{N}
in Section
2.6
. As we can define
\mathbb{N}
in
\mathbb{S}
, these codes are available to us in
\mathbb{S}
as well. Now we would like to map the codes
a
of the real algebraic numbers to the real algebraic number as they occur
in
\mathbb{S}
. However, a real algebraic number
\alpha
that is not a natural number is not a first-class citizen in
\mathbb{S}
; such a number
\alpha
only occurs
in contexts like
\alpha\hskip 0.71114pt{\sf X}^{q}
, where
q\in\mathbb{Q}^{{>0}}
. To solve this problem, we map an
a\in\omega_{{\mathbb{S}}}
that represents
\alpha\in{\sf rc}(\mathbb{Q})
(as in our adopted coding of real closures discussed in Subsection
2.6
) in combination
with
q,r\in\omega_{{\mathbb{S}}}
to first-class citizen
\alpha\hskip 0.71114pt{\sf X}^{q/r}
.
Let
a={\langle p,k\rangle}
and
p={\langle z_{n},\ldots,z_{0}\rangle}
.
We define:

Q_{x,p}({\sf Y}):=z_{n}{\sf Y}^{n}+z_{n-1}{\sf Y}^{n-1}x+\ldots+z_{1}{\sf Y}x^{n-1}+z_{0}x^{n}.

We can now define an application function
{\sf app}^{\ast}
in analogy of
app
in Section
4.5
,
where
{\sf app}_{x}^{\ast}(p,y)=Q_{x,p}(y)
.

Clearly,
\alpha
is a solution of

P_{p}({\sf Y})=0
in
Y
iff
\alpha\hskip 0.71114pt{\sf X}^{q}
is a solution of
Q_{{\sf X}^{q},p}({\sf Y})=0
in
Y
. Moreover, the order of the corresponding solutions is preserved.

Let
a:={\langle p,k\rangle}
. We define the partial function
{\sf ev}_{z}
that is chosen in such a way that

{\sf ev}_{{\sf X}^{q}}(a)=\alpha{\sf X}^{q}
, where
q\geqslant 0
.

\bullet

{\sf ev}_{z}(a)=w
iff there is an
s\in{\sf seq}
such that:

\circ

{\sf length}(s)=k
,

\circ

\pi(s,k-1)=w
,

\circ

for all
i<k
, we have
{\sf app}^{\ast}_{z}(p,\pi(s,i))=0
,

\circ

for all
i<j<k
, we have
\pi(s,i)<\pi(s,j)
,

\circ

if
y\leqslant w
and

{\sf app}^{\ast}_{z}(p,y)=0
, then, for some
j<k
, we have
\pi(s,j)=y
.

We note that
{\sf ev}
is a partial function. If we apply it to the correct inputs where
a
is
a representation of an algebraic number and
z={\sf X}^{q}
, for
q\in\mathbb{Q}^{{\geqslant 0}}
, then it will
deliver the desired value.

5.4.
Interpreting
\mathbb{N}
in
\mathbb{S}

As in the case of
{\sf PA}^{-}
, the interpretation of
\mathbb{N}
in
\mathbb{S}
, denoted
{\sf N_{{\mathbb{S}}}}
,
is given by restricting the domain of
\mathbb{S}
to
\omega_{{\mathbb{S}}}
.

5.5.
Interpreting
\mathbb{S}
in
\mathbb{N}

We construct an isomorphic copy
\mathbb{S}^{\ast}
of
\mathbb{S}
in
\mathbb{N}
.

An element of
\mathbb{S}
will be represented via its standard form, that is as a (code of a) sequence of pairs

\langle{\langle a_{0},0\rangle},{\langle a_{1},q_{1}\rangle}\ldots,{\langle a_{n},q_{n}\rangle}\rangle
, where the
a_{i}
are representatives of real algebraic numbers,
q_{i}
’s are rational numbers both satisfying the criteria for the standard form. We shall often implicitely transform this representation to a pair of sequences, denoted
{\langle\bar{a},\bar{q}\rangle}
.
Identity, the ordering, zero, one, addition and multiplication on these objects can be defined as a matter of course. We call our interpretation
S
.

5.6.
The Isomorphism

Finally, let us give the formula
\digamma_{z}(x,y)
, which will define the isomorphism for
z:{\sf X}
, from the

\mathbb{N}
-internal model representing
\mathbb{S}
to
\mathbb{S}
.
We define:

\bullet

\digamma_{z}(x,y)
iff
x={\langle\bar{a},\bar{q}\rangle}
is an
\mathbb{N}
-representation of an element of
\mathbb{S}
and

y=\sum_{j<{\sf length}(x)}{\sf ev}_{z^{q_{j}}}(a_{j})
.

Here the sum is defined in the obvious way by recursion, which we do have since we have standardly finite sequences.

Note that
\digamma_{z}(x,y)
is equivalent to a
\Sigma_{1}
-formula.

Theorem 5.2
.

There is a completion of
IOpen
that is axiomatisable by a single sentence together with an infinite set of
\Sigma_{1}
-sentences.

Proof.

This follows immediately from Corollary
3.4
with:

\bullet

\mathbb{S}
in the role of
\mathcal{M}
,

\bullet

\leqslant
in the role of
\sqsubset
,

\bullet

\mathbb{N}
in the role of

\mathcal{N}
,

\bullet

\mathsf{N}_{\mathbb{S}}
in the role of
\imath
,

\bullet

S
in the role of
\jmath
.

\bullet

\omega_{{\mathbb{S}}}^{\sf c}
in the role of
\theta
.

\bullet

\digamma_{{\sf X}}(x,y)
is an
(\mathbb{S},{\sf X})
-definable isomorphism between

\mathbb{S}^{\ast}
and
\mathbb{S}
.
So, the statement that, for some
z
, the function
\digamma_{z}
is an isomorphism
delivers our desired witness of an
{\sf INT}^{\sf fw}_{3}
-retract.

❑

Shepherdson’s construction of the model
\mathbb{S}
led to a substantial body of work on the construction of other recursive models of
\mathsf{IOpen}
that satisfy desirable algebraic properties, such as normality (which implies that
\sqrt{2}
is irrational), the Bezout property, the GCD property, and having unboundely many primes, all of which fail in
\mathbb{S}
. Mohsenipour’s paper
[
Moh06
]
contains the latest results in this direction
12
12

12

It is easy to see that the ring associated with
\mathbb{S}
is not a factorization domain since
{\sf X}
can be written as the product of
\frac{1}{2}{\sf X}
and
2
in
\mathbb{S}
, neither of which is a unit (invertible). So here there are no significant free riders.
This prompts the following question, which we suspect has a positive answer.

Open Question 5.3
.

Is there a model of
\mathsf{IOpen}
whose complete theory has a restricted axiomatization, and which satisfies normality and other desirable algebraic properties?
❍

6.
A restricted Completion of
{\sf PA}^{-}
plus full Collection

In this section, we present a model of
{\sf PA}^{-}+{\sf Coll}
whose complete theory is axiomatized by a collection of axioms consisting of a single sentence together with an infinite number of
\Sigma_{1}
-sentences. Note that
{\sf Coll}
fails in the models
\mathbb{M}
and
\mathbb{S}
(using sequentiality, the definability of the standard cut in both models, and the fact that in both models finite powers of
{\sf X}
are cofinal).

6.1.
Basics of the Model
\mathbb{A}

Let
\mathbb{Q}
be the ordered set of rationals. In this section we develop the basic algebraic properties of the discrete ordered semi-ring
\mathbb{A}
, defined as follows:

\mathbb{A}:={\hat{\mathbb{A}}}^{{\geqslant 0}},~\textrm{where~}\hat{\mathbb{A}}=\mathbb{Z}[{\sf X}_{q}\mathop{\mid}q\in\mathbb{Q}].

In the above each
{\sf X}_{q}
is an indeterminate, thus
\hat{\mathbb{A}}
is a multivariable polynomial ring.
The ordering on
\hat{\mathbb{A}}
is obtained by treating each
{\sf X}_{q}
as infinite, and stipulating that each
{\sf X}_{q}
dominates all polynomial
expressions whose indeterminates come from
\{{\sf X}_{q^{\prime}}:q^{\prime}<q\}
. The model
\mathbb{A}
was introduced in
[
EL24
]
, where it was
shown to satisfy
\mathsf{PA^{-}+Coll}.
13
13

13

It has been known for some time that

\mathbb{Z}[{\sf X}_{\alpha}\mathop{\mid}\alpha\in\omega_{1}]^{{\geqslant 0}}
is a model
\mathsf{PA^{-}+Coll}
in
which the standard cut is definable (here
\omega_{1}
be the first uncountable ordinal). See Exercise 7.7 of Kaye’s
textbook
[
Kay91
, Chapter 7.2]
. However, in contrast to
\mathbb{A}
, this model cannot be interpreted in
\mathbb{N}
since it is uncountable.

We present an alternative description of
\hat{\mathbb{A}}
using the model-theoretic notion of
directed union
.
14
14

14

The directed union construction is a special
case of the
direct limit
, in which the embeddings that “glue” the constituent pieces of the model together are inclusion maps.

This might strike the reader as spurious duplication, but we
find it useful in developing a ‘geometric’ intuition about the model, and also because it
provides a dress rehearsal for the analysis of the far more complicated model
\mathbb{B}
of
\mathsf{IOpen+Coll}
presented in Section
7
.

Recall that a partially ordered set
\mathbb{D}
is said to be
directed
if any pair of elements of
\mathbb{D}
have a common upper bound. Given a structure
\mathcal{M}
, a directed set
\mathbb{D}
, and an indexed collection
\{\mathcal{M}_{d}:d\in\mathbb{D}\}
of submodels of
\mathcal{M}
,
\mathcal{M}
is said to be a
directed union
of
\{\mathcal{M}_{d}:d\in\mathbb{B}\}
, if

1
.

\mathcal{M=}\bigcup\limits_{d\in\mathbb{D}}\mathcal{M}_{d}
, and

2
.

\mathcal{M}_{d}

is a submodel of
\mathcal{M}_{d^{\prime}}
for
d\leqslant_{\mathbb{D}}d^{\prime}.

Given an ordered ring
\mathcal{R}
and an indeterminate
{\sf X}
, we use
\mathcal{R}[{\sf X}]
to refer to the ordered ring of polynomials with coefficients in
\mathcal{R}
, where
{\sf X}
is greater than all elements of
\mathcal{R}
, precisely as in the special case where
\mathcal{R}=\mathbb{Z}
, discussed in Subsection
4.1
.

Let
[\mathbb{Q}]^{<\omega}
be the directed set of finite subsets of
\mathbb{Q}
(ordered by inclusion). For
Y\in[\mathbb{Q}]^{<\omega}
, the ordered ring
\hat{\mathbb{A}}(Y)
is constructed by the following clauses:

\bullet

\hat{\mathbb{A}}({\varnothing}):=\mathbb{Z}
, and

\bullet

\hat{\mathbb{A}}({Y\cup\{q\}}):=\hat{\mathbb{A}}(Y)[{\sf X}_{q}]
, where
q^{\prime}<q
for all
q^{\prime}\in Y.

Officially speaking,

\hat{\mathbb{A}}:=\bigcup\limits_{Y\in[\mathbb{Q}]^{<\omega}}\hat{\mathbb{A}}(Y).

Clearly,
\hat{\mathbb{A}}(Y)
is a submodel of
\hat{\mathbb{A}}(Y^{\prime})
for
Y\subseteq Y^{\prime}
. Coupled with the fact
[\mathbb{Q}]^{<\omega}
is a directed set under containment, we have the following observation:

Lemma 6.1
.

\hat{\mathbb{A}}
is a directed union of its constituent submodels
\hat{\mathbb{A}}(Y)
, as
Y
ranges in
[\mathbb{Q}]^{<\omega}
.

Therefore, based on classical facts about direct limits (as in
[
Hod93
, Section 2.3]
), if
\varphi
is a sentence in the language of ordered rings that can be written in the form
\forall\exists
, and
\varphi
holds in
\hat{\mathbb{A}}(Y)
for all
Y\in[\mathbb{Q}]^{<\omega}
,
then
\varphi
holds in
\hat{\mathbb{A}}
as well. This makes it clear that
\hat{\mathbb{A}}
is a discretely ordered ring, which in turn shows that its non-negative part, i.e.,
\mathbb{A}
, satisfies
\mathsf{PA^{-}}
.
15
15

15

It is straightforward to verify that that if all the constituent models
\mathcal{M}_{d}
of a directed union
\mathcal{M}
satisfy a sentence
\varphi
that can be put in the form
\forall\exists
, then
\mathcal{M}\models\varphi.
The classical Chang-Suzko theorem shows that the converse is also true, but we don’t need this fact.

Definition 6.2
.

The
support
of an element
p\in\hat{\mathbb{A}}
, written
\mathsf{supp}(p)
, is the smallest
Y\in[\mathbb{Q}]^{<\omega}
(in the sense of inclusion) such that
p\in\hat{\mathbb{A}}(Y)
. Standard arguments show that
\mathsf{supp}(p)
is well-defined (see Section
C
of the Appendix).
❍

It will be useful to have names for certain subrings of
\hat{\mathbb{A}}
, and certain initial segments of
\mathbb{A}
.

Definition 6.3
.

In what follows, given
q\in\mathbb{Q}
, we take
\mathbb{Q}_{q}:=\{q^{\prime}\in\mathbb{Q}\mathop{\mid}q^{\prime}<q\}
.

\bullet

\hat{\mathbb{A}}_{q}:=\mathbb{Z}[{\sf X}_{q^{\prime}}\mathop{\mid}q^{\prime}\in\mathbb{Q}_{q}]~=\bigcup\limits_{Y\in[\mathbb{Q}_{q}]^{<\omega}}\hat{\mathbb{A}}(Y)
, and

\bullet

\mathbb{A}_{q}:=\hat{\mathbb{A}}_{q}^{{\geqslant 0}}.

❍

It can be readily checked that
\mathbb{A}_{q}\subsetneq_{\mathrm{end}}\mathbb{A}_{q^{\prime}}
whenever
q<q^{\prime}
. Indeed, as shown in Theorem
6.4
,
\mathbb{A}_{q}\prec_{\mathrm{end}}\mathbb{A}
for each
q\in\mathbb{Q}
, which implies that
\mathbb{A}_{q}\prec_{\mathrm{end}}\mathbb{A}_{q^{\prime}}
for
q<q^{\prime}
.

Note that for
q<q^{\prime}
we have:

\mathbb{A}_{q}\subsetneq_{\mathrm{end}}\hat{\mathbb{A}}_{q}[{\sf X}_{q}]^{{\geqslant 0}}\subsetneq_{\mathrm{end}}\mathbb{A}_{q^{\prime}}.

We also observe that
\{{\sf X}_{q}^{n}\mathop{\mid}n\in\mathbb{N}\}
is cofinal in
\hat{\mathbb{A}}_{q}[{\sf X}_{q}]^{{\geqslant 0}}
, and
\{{\sf X}_{q}-n\mathop{\mid}n\in\mathbb{N}\}
is downward cofinal in
\hat{\mathbb{A}}_{q}[{\sf X}_{q}]^{{\geqslant 0}}\setminus\mathbb{A}_{q}
.

6.2.
\mathbb{A}
is a model of
{\sf PA}^{-}
plus full Collection

We have already explained why
\mathbb{A}
satisfies
\mathsf{PA^{-}}
. We now verify that
\mathbb{A}
also satisfies

Coll
. This result was first proved in
[
EL24
, Corollary 34]
.

Recall that for
q\in\mathbb{Q}
,
\mathbb{A}_{q}
is the initial segment of
\mathbb{A}
that consists of elements whose support is a subset of
\{{\sf X}_{q^{\prime}}\mathop{\mid}q^{\prime}<q\}
.

Theorem 6.4
.

For each
q\in\mathbb{Q}
the model
\mathbb{A}_{q}
is a downwards closed cut of
\mathbb{A}
which is closed under the arithmetical
operations. Moreover,
\mathbb{A}_{q}\prec_{\mathrm{end}}\mathbb{A}
.

Proof.

We only prove
\mathbb{A}_{q}\prec\mathbb{A}

since it is straightforward to verify that
\mathbb{A}_{q}
is a downwards closed cut of
\mathbb{A}
. Consider a formula

\varphi(p_{0},\cdots,p_{k-1})
, where
p_{0},\cdots,p_{k-1}
are parameters from
\mathbb{A}_{q}.
Since the support of each
polynomial is finite, there is some
q^{\prime}<q
such that
\mathbb{Q}_{q^{\prime}}
contains the support of each
p_{i}
. By a standard argument there is an order
isomorphism
f:\mathbb{Q}_{q}\rightarrow\mathbb{Q}
such that
f(x)=x
for each
x<q^{\prime}
. This isomorphism naturally induces an
isomorphism
F:\mathbb{A}_{q}\rightarrow\mathbb{A}
such that
F
fixes each element of
\mathbb{A}_{q^{\prime}}
.
Since the isomorphism
F
fixes each
p_{i}
, this makes it clear that

\mathbb{A}_{q}\models\varphi(p_{0},\cdots,p_{k-1})~\mathrm{iff~}\mathbb{A}\models\varphi(p_{0},\cdot\cdot\cdot,p_{k-1})
.
❑

Theorem 6.5
.

\mathbb{A}
satisfies
Coll
.

Proof.

Consider any
q\in\mathbb{Q}
. By Theorem
6.4
, (†)
\mathbb{A}_{q}\prec_{\mathrm{end}}\mathbb{A}
.
So it suffices to show that
\mathbb{A}_{q}
satisfies
Coll
.
Suppose we have
\mathbb{A}_{q}\models\forall x\mathbin{<}a\,\exists y~\varphi(x,y,\mathchoice{\vbox{\halign{#\cr\kern-0.7pt\cr$\mkern . So, again by (†),

\mathbb{A}_{q}\models\exists w\,\forall x\mathbin{<}a\,\exists y\mathbin{<}w~\varphi(x,y,\mathchoice{\vbox{\halign{#\cr\kern-0.7pt\cr$\mkern 2.0mu\displaystyle\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}}\mkern-1.5mu\leaders{\hbox{$\displaystyle\mkern 0.0mu\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\montraita}$}}}\mkern 0.0mu$}}{\hfill}\mkern-1.5mu\fldr$\crcr\kern-0.3pt\cr$\hfil\displaystyle b\hfil$\crcr}}}{\vbox{\halign{#\cr\kern-0.7pt\cr$\mkern 2.0mu\textstyle\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}}\mkern-1.5mu\leaders{\hbox{$\textstyle\mkern 0.0mu\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\montraita}$}}}\mkern 0.0mu$}}{\hfill}\mkern-1.5mu\fldr$\crcr\kern-0.3pt\cr$\hfil\textstyle b\hfil$\crcr}}}{\vbox{\halign{#\cr\kern-0.7pt\cr$\mkern 2.0mu\scriptstyle\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}}\mkern-1.5mu\leaders{\hbox{$\scriptstyle\mkern 0.0mu\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\montraita}$}}}\mkern 0.0mu$}}{\hfill}\mkern-1.5mu\fldr$\crcr\kern-0.3pt\cr$\hfil\scriptstyle b\hfil$\crcr}}}{\vbox{\halign{#\cr\kern-0.7pt\cr$\mkern 2.0mu\scriptscriptstyle\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}}\mkern-1.5mu\leaders{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\mkern 0.0mu\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\montraita}$}}}\mkern 0.0mu$}}{\hfill}\mkern-1.5mu\fldr$\crcr\kern-0.3pt\cr$\hfil\scriptscriptstyle b\hfil$\crcr}}})
.
❑

Remark 6.6
.

The reasoning used in the proof of Theorem
6.5
shows, more generally, that
if
\mathcal{M}
is an ordered structure that has an elementary initial segment
(
or an elementary end extension
)
,
then the collection scheme holds in
\mathcal{M}
.
16
16

16

This fact is well-known and due to Keisler,
who also showed, using an omitting types theorem, that if
\mathcal{M}
is a countable ordered structure
in a countable language (signature) such that
\mathcal{M}
satisfies the collection scheme,
then
\mathcal{M}
has an elementary end extension.

6.3.
Standard forms and pseudo-variables

We need the notion of
standard form
for elements of
\hat{\mathbb{A}}
. We say that the expression

a_{n}{\sf X}_{q_{n}}^{k_{n}}+a_{n-1}{\sf X}_{q_{n-1}}^{k_{n-1}}+\dots+a_{0}

is the standard form of
x\in\hat{\mathbb{A}}
, if
x=a_{n}{\sf X}_{q_{n}}^{k_{n}}+a_{n-1}{\sf X}_{q_{n-1}}^{k_{n-1}}+\dots+a_{0}
, and the following conditions are satisfied (note that (5) is a recursive condition).

1
.

a_{i}\neq 0
for
0<i\leqslant n
.

2
.

k_{i}\in\mathbb{N}^{{>0}}
for
0<i\leqslant n
.

3
.

a_{0}\in\mathbb{Z}
.

4
.

a_{i}\in\hat{\mathsf{A}}_{q_{i}}
for
0<i\leqslant n
.

5
.

a_{i}
is itself in standard form for
0<i\leqslant n
.

6
.

q_{i+1}\geqslant q_{i}
and, if

q_{i+1}=q_{i}
, then
k_{i+1}>k_{i}
; here
0<i<n.

A standard form expression
a_{n}{\sf X}_{q_{n}}^{k_{n}}+a_{n-1}{\sf X}_{q_{n-1}}^{k_{n-1}}+\dots+a_{0}

is the standard form of an element of

\mathbb{A}
if
a_{n}\geqslant 0
.

Observe that if
x\in\hat{\mathbb{A}}
, then
{\sf supp}(x)
consists of the
{\sf X}_{q}
such that
{\sf X}_{q}
occurs in the standard form of
x
. Also, note that if
x
divides
x^{\prime}
in
\hat{\mathbb{A}}
, then
{\sf supp}(x)\subseteq{\sf supp}(x^{\prime})
.
This observation immediately shows that we have unique factorisation in prime factors in
\hat{\mathbb{A}}
,
since factorisation in the case of
y
reduces to factorisation in
\mathbb{Z}[{\sf supp}(y)]
.

Suppose
a_{n}{\sf X}_{q_{n}}^{k_{n}}+\dots a_{0}
is the standard form of
x\in\mathbb{A}
.

We define:

\bullet

{\sf rank}_{0}(x):=q_{n}
, if
n>0
and
{\sf rank}_{0}(x):=-\infty
if
n=0
. .

\bullet

{\sf rank}(x):=(q_{n},k_{n})
, if

n>0
and
{\sf rank}_{0}(x):=(-\infty,0)
if
n=0
.
17
17

17

Here we use
(x,y)
for the formation of the ordered pair of
x
and
y
in the metalanguage.

\bullet

(q,k)<(r,m)
iff
q<r
or (
q=r
and
k<m
).

We say
y{\sf X}_{q}^{k}+z
is the
basic form
of
x\in\mathbb{A}
, if

x=y{\sf X}_{q}^{k}+z
and
{\sf rank}_{0}(y)<q
and
{\sf rank}(|z|)<(q,k)
.

We now give intuitions about the above definitions. Observe that for
x\in\mathbb{A}
,
{\sf rank}_{0}(x):=q
iff
q
is the
least element of

\{q^{\prime}\in\mathbb{Q}\mathop{\mid}x\in\hat{\mathbb{A}}_{q^{\prime}}[{\sf X}_{q^{\prime}}]^{{\geqslant 0}}\}.

Thus, if
{\sf rank}_{0}(x):=q
, then
x
can be written in the basic form
y{\sf X}_{q}^{k}+z
, where

y\in\mathbb{A}_{q}
, and
|z|
is a polynomial in
\mathbb{A}_{q}[{\sf X}_{q}]
of degree less than
k
.

We observe that
\hat{\mathbb{A}}
is a Dorroh ring, as witnessed by the ideal consisting of
x\in\hat{\mathbb{A}}
such that
a_{0}=0
, where
x
is written in standard form.
Thus,
\omega
is definable in
\mathbb{A}
. For
x
and
y
in
\mathbb{A}
let us write:

\bullet

y\ll x
for
\forall n\mathbin{\in}\omega\;(y+2)^{n}<x
.

\bullet

{\sf pvar}(x):\leftrightarrow\forall y\mathbin{\leqslant}x~(y\ll x\vee(x-y)\ll x).

We refer to the elements of
\mathbb{A}
satisfying
{\sf pvar}
as the
pseudo-variables
. This terminology is motivated by
Theorem
6.9
which shows that the collection of pseudovariables ‘behaves like’ the collection of indeterminates in
\mathbb{A}
.

Theorem 6.7
.

For all
x
and
y
in
\mathbb{A}
we have:
y\ll x
iff
{\sf rank}_{0}(y)<{\sf rank}_{0}(x)
.

Proof.

Let
q^{\ast}:={\sf rank}_{0}(x)
and

r^{\ast}:={\sf rank}_{0}(y)
.
It follows that
x
has basic form
z{\sf X}^{n}_{q^{\ast}}+w
,
and
y
has basic form
u{\sf X}^{m}_{r^{\ast}}+v
.

Suppose
y\ll x
.
Let
mk>n
.
We find
y^{k}<x
, so
r^{\ast}<q^{\ast}
.

Conversely, if
r^{\ast}<q^{\ast}
, it is easy to see that for all
n
,
y^{n}<x
.
❑

Theorem 6.8
.

For each
x\in\mathbb{A}
,
{\sf pvar}(x)
holds iff
x={\sf X}_{q}+y
, where
{\sf X}_{q}+y
is in basic form

(
so,
|y|\ll{\sf X}_{q}
)
.

Proof.

Clearly, no element of
\omega_{\mathbb{A}}
satisfies
{\sf pvar}
.

Suppose
x
has basic form
z{\sf X}_{q}^{n}+y
.
If
z>1
or
n>1
, neither
{\sf X}_{q}\ll x
nor
x-{\sf X}_{q}\ll x
.
So, if
x
is in

pvar
, we must have
x={\sf X}_{q}+y
, where
|y|\ll{\sf X}_{q}
.

Conversely, suppose
x={\sf X}_{q}+y

Suppose
z\not\ll x
. Then,
z^{m}>x
, for some
m
. It follows that
z
must have basic form
u{\sf X}_{r}^{k}+v
,
where
r\geqslant q
. If
u>1
or
r>q
or
k>1
, we find
x-z=0
and we are done. Suppose
z={\sf X}_{q}+v
.
It follows that
x-z=y-v\leqslant y\ll x
.
❑

6.4.
Interpreting
\mathbb{N}
in
\mathbb{A}

Recall that
\mathbb{A}=\hat{\mathbb{A}}^{{\geqslant 0}}
. Since
\hat{\mathbb{A}}
is clearly an ordered Dorroh ring, the standard cut
\omega_{{\mathbb{A}}}
of
\mathbb{A}
is definable in
\mathbb{A}
by Theorem
2.9
. This gives us an interpretation, say
N
, of
\mathbb{N}
in
\mathbb{A}
. Note that
N
is just domain restriction to
\omega_{{\mathbb{A}}}
.

6.5.
Interpreting
\mathbb{A}
in
\mathbb{N}

Inside
\mathbb{N}
, we can build a copy of
\mathbb{A}
in some
standard way. This gives rise to an interpretation
A
of
\mathbb{A}
in
\mathbb{N}
.

We use
\alpha
for the underlying translation of
{\sf A}\circ{\sf N}
.
Thus, we will call the internal copy
\mathbb{A}^{\alpha}
.

We will use
{\mathfrak{F}}
for the canonical isomorphism from
\mathbb{A}^{\alpha}
to
\mathbb{A}
. We note that
this isomorphism is not
\mathbb{A}
-internally definable.

6.6.
Automorphisms

In this section, we acquire some information on automorphisms of
\mathbb{A}
.

Theorem 6.9
.

Suppose
x_{0}\ll x_{1}\ll\dots\ll x_{n-1}
and
y_{0}\ll y_{1}\ll\dots\ll y_{n-1}
, where the
x_{i}
and
y_{i}

are elements of
\mathbb{A}
that are pseudo-variables. Then, there is an
\mathbb{A}
-automorphism
\sigma
that sends
x_{i}
to
y_{i}
.

Proof.

Clearly, it is sufficient to prove this for the case that
x_{i}={\sf X}_{q_{i}}
, where
q_{i}<q_{j}
if
i<j
.
Moreover, if we have
q_{0}<q_{1}\dots<q_{n-1}
and
r_{0}<r_{1}\dots<r_{n-1}
, then there is
an order-automorphism
\theta
of
\mathbb{Q}
such that
\theta(r_{i})=q_{i}
.
So, we have an automorphism
\tau
of
\mathbb{A}
that moves
{\sf X}_{r_{i}}
to
{\sf X}_{q_{i}}
.
By Theorem
6.8
, it follows that it suffices to consider the case where
x_{i}={\sf X}_{q_{i}}
and
y_{i}={\sf X}_{q_{i}}+a_{i}
.
We assume this simplification.

We take
\sigma(z):=z[x_{0}:y_{0},\dots,x_{n-1}:y_{n-1}]
. Here, of course,
\sigma
operates identically on all
variables distinct from the
x_{i}
. This is clearly a morphism. It suffices to define
an inverse morphism.

We define the inverse
\mu
by:

\bullet

\mu
operates identically on all variables not among the
{\sf X}_{q_{i}}
.

\bullet

\mu({\sf X}_{q_{i}}):={\sf X}_{q_{i}}-\mu(a_{i})
, for
i<n
.

We note that this is not circular since
a_{i}
only contains
variables of lower index.

We now have, assuming that we have the desired result already for
j<i
:

\displaystyle\sigma\mu({\sf X}_{q_{i}})

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\sigma({\sf X}_{q_{i}}-\mu(a_{i}))

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\sigma({\sf X}_{q_{i}})-\sigma\mu(a_{i})

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle{\sf X}_{q_{i}}+a_{i}-a_{i}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle{\sf X}_{q_{i}}

and

\displaystyle\mu\sigma({\sf X}_{q_{i}})

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\mu({\sf X}_{q_{i}}+a_{i})

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\mu({\sf X}_{q_{i}})+\mu(a_{i})

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle{\sf X}_{q_{i}}-\mu(a_{i})+\mu(a_{i})

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle{\sf X}_{q_{i}}.

We note that we used our assumption that we have the result for
j<i
to conclude that
\sigma\mu(a_{i})=a_{i}
.
❑

Open Question 6.10
.

We note that we did not give a characterisation of all automorphisms. Thus, the question of such a characterisation remains open.
❍

6.7.
Extension Lemma

In this section, we prove an important lemma that will help to verify the
correctness of the Ehrenfeucht-Fraïssé game that we will specify.

For a subset
W
of
\mathbb{A}
consisting of pseudo-variables, we say that
W
is
independent
when no two elements of
W
are finitely apart, i.e., when no two elements of
W
have the same
\mathsf{rank}_{0}
. We define the support of a set as the union of the supports of its elements.

Lemma 6.11
.

Let
W
be an independent finite set of pseudo-variables. Then, there is a finite set of variables
V
so that

W\cup V
is independent and
{\sf supp}(W)
is polynomially definable from
W\cup V
.

Proof.

Consider an independent finite subset of pseudo-variables
W
. Let

W
be
\{w_{0},\dots w_{k-1}\}
, where
{\sf rank}_{0}(w_{i})<{\sf rank}_{0}(w_{i+1})
.
Let

W^{\star}:=\{{\sf X}_{q_{i}}\mathop{\mid}{\sf rank}_{0}(w_{i})=q_{i}\text{ and }i<k\}.

Let

V:={\sf supp}(W)\setminus W^{\star}
and let
Z:=W\cup V
. We note that
Z
is independent.

We have to show that the elements of
{\sf supp}(W)
are polynomially definable from
Z
.
It clearly suffices to show that the elements of
W^{\star}
are polynomially definable from
Z
.
Let these elements be
{\sf X}_{q_{1}},\dots,{\sf X}_{q_{k-1}}
.
Suppose, for each
j<i
, the element
{\sf X}_{q_{i}}
is polynomially definable from
Z
.
We have
w_{i}={\sf X}_{q_{i}}+b_{i}
, where
b_{i}
contains at most elements of
{\sf supp}(W)\setminus W^{\star}
plus
elements
{\sf X}_{q_{j}}
, for
j<i
. All these are definable from
Z
by our assumption.
This tells us that
b_{i}
and, hence,
{\sf X}_{q_{i}}
is definable from
Z
.
By course of values induction, we are done.
❑

Theorem 6.12
.

Suppose
W
is an independent finite set of pseudo-variables in
\mathbb{A}

and suppose
x
is any element in
\mathbb{A}
.
Then, there is an independent finite set of pseudo-variables
U
in
\mathbb{A}
such that

W\subseteq U
and
x
is polynomially definable from
U
.

Proof.

Let
W
be an independent finite set of pseudo-variables in
\mathbb{A}
and let
x
be any element of
\mathbb{A}
.
Let
V
be as promised in Lemma
6.11
for
W
.
We take
U:=W\cup V\cup({\sf supp}(x)\setminus{\sf supp}(W))
. Since
V
consists of variables,

U
is independent and, since
{\sf supp}(W)
is polynomially definable from
W\cup V
,
every element of
{\sf supp}(x)
is polynomially definable from
U
.
❑

We note that Theorem
6.12
is a first-order statement in
\mathbb{A}
.

6.8.
An Ehrenfeucht-Fraïssé Game

Recall from Subsection
6.5
that
\alpha
is the translation that delivers the
\mathbb{A}
-internal copy
\mathbb{A}^{\alpha}
of
\mathbb{A}
. Clearly,
\mathbb{A}\models\varphi\leftrightarrow\varphi^{\alpha}
, for all sentences
\varphi
.
However, we want to make
the argument for this fact
\mathbb{A}
-internal, in such a way that we can extract a finite subtheory
\varPhi
of

{\sf Th}(\mathbb{A})
, such
\varPhi\vdash\varphi\leftrightarrow\varphi^{\alpha}
, for all sentences
\varphi
. We note that
the isomorphism, say
{\mathfrak{F}}
, from
\mathbb{A}^{\alpha}
to
\mathbb{A}
is not
\mathbb{A}
-definable,
so we need something else. We develop an Ehrenfeucht-Fraïssé game between
\mathbb{A}^{\alpha}
and

\mathbb{A}
.

In this subsection, we will use
x,y,\dots
for general
\mathbb{A}
-elements and
u,v,\dots
for

\mathbb{A}^{\alpha}
-elements

We assume that the
\mathbb{A}^{\alpha}
-elements
u
are represented by (something like)
numerical codes of their canonical representations by polynomials.
We note that
\mathbb{A}^{\alpha}
is entirely constructed inside the internal copy of
\omega
.
Inside
\omega
we can, of course, also define things like the support function for
the elements of
\mathbb{A}^{\alpha}
qua coded polynomials, even if the real support function for

\mathbb{A}
is not
\mathbb{A}
-definable (since
\mathbb{A}
has lots of automorphisms that send variables to pseudo-variables).

We will think of
\alpha
as a translation that also works
on an extended signature for
\mathbb{A}
which includes the variables and the support function.
Thus, we will write
{\sf X}_{q}^{\alpha}
and
{\sf supp}^{\alpha}
for the
\mathbb{A}^{\alpha}
-internal variables
and the
\mathbb{A}^{\alpha}
-support function.

We define the promised Ehrenfeucht-Fraïssé game.
We say that a finite function
F
is a
witnessing function
if it has as domain a finite set of the
{\sf X}^{\alpha}_{q}
and as range a
set of pseudo-variables with the constraint that, if
{\sf X}^{\alpha}_{q}
and
{\sf X}^{\alpha}_{q^{\prime}}
are in the domain and if
q<q^{\prime}
, then

F({\sf X}^{\alpha}_{q})\ll F({\sf X}^{\alpha}_{q^{\prime}})
.

Clearly, we can define
{\sf ev}_{F}(u)
a function that evaluates
u
qua coded
canonical polynomial by giving the variables
{\sf X}^{\alpha}_{q}
the evaluation
F({\sf X}^{\alpha}_{q})
.

Suppose
G
is a finite function from
\mathbb{A}^{\alpha}
to
\mathbb{A}
.
We say that
G
is
good
if, there is a witnessing function
F
, such that

{\sf dom}(F)\supseteq{\sf supp}^{\alpha}({\sf dom}(G))
and, for all
u\in{\sf dom}(G)
,
we have
G(u)={\sf ev}_{F}(u)
.

Suppose
P(\mathchoice{\vbox{\halign{#\cr\kern-0.7pt\cr$\mkern 2.0mu\displaystyle\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}}\mkern-1.5mu\leaders{\hbox{$\displaystyle\mkern 0.0mu\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\montraita}$}}}\mkern 0.0mu$}}{\hfill}\mkern-1.5mu\fldr$\crcr\kern-0.3pt\cr$\hfil\displaystyle x\hfil$\crcr}}}{\vbox{\halign{#\cr\kern-0.7pt\cr$\mkern 2.0mu\textstyle\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}}\mkern-1.5mu\leaders{\hbox{$\textstyle\mkern 0.0mu\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\montraita}$}}}\mkern 0.0mu$}}{\hfill}\mkern-1.5mu\fldr$\crcr\kern-0.3pt\cr$\hfil\textstyle x\hfil$\crcr}}}{\vbox{\halign{#\cr\kern-0.7pt\cr$\mkern 2.0mu\scriptstyle\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}}\mkern-1.5mu\leaders{\hbox{$\scriptstyle\mkern 0.0mu\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\montraita}$}}}\mkern 0.0mu$}}{\hfill}\mkern-1.5mu\fldr$\crcr\kern-0.3pt\cr$\hfil\scriptstyle x\hfil$\crcr}}}{\vbox{\halign{#\cr\kern-0.7pt\cr$\mkern 2.0mu\scriptscriptstyle\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}}\mkern-1.5mu\leaders{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\mkern 0.0mu\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\montraita}$}}}\mkern 0.0mu$}}{\hfill}\mkern-1.5mu\fldr$\crcr\kern-0.3pt\cr$\hfil\scriptscriptstyle x\hfil$\crcr}}})
is one of
x=y
,
x\leqslant y
,
{\sf Z}(x)
,
{\sf S}(x,y)
,
{\sf A}(x,y,z)
,
{\sf M}(x,y,z)
.
Here, e.g.,
A
is the relational version of addition. We have:

Lemma 6.13

(Atomic Clause)
.

Consider the formula
P
and a sequence

\mkern 2.0mu\textstyle\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}}\mkern-1.5mu\leaders{\hbox{$\textstyle\mkern 0.0mu\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\montraita}$}}}\mkern 0.0mu$}}{\hfill}\mkern-1.5mu\fldr

\hfil\textstyle u\hfil

whose length equals the arity of
P
. In
\mathbb{A}
, we have the following. Suppose
G
is a good function and the domain of

G
extends

\mkern 2.0mu\textstyle\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}}\mkern-1.5mu\leaders{\hbox{$\textstyle\mkern 0.0mu\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\montraita}$}}}\mkern 0.0mu$}}{\hfill}\mkern-1.5mu\fldr

\hfil\textstyle u\hfil

.
Then, we have
P^{\alpha}(\mathchoice{\vbox{\halign{#\cr\kern-0.7pt\cr$\mkern 2.0mu\displaystyle\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}}\mkern-1.5mu\leaders{\hbox{$\displaystyle\mkern 0.0mu\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\montraita}$}}}\mkern 0.0mu$}}{\hfill}\mkern-1.5mu\fldr$\crcr\kern-0.3pt\cr$\hfil\displaystyle u\hfil$\crcr}}}{\vbox{\halign{#\cr\kern-0.7pt\cr$\mkern 2.0mu\textstyle\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}}\mkern-1.5mu\leaders{\hbox{$\textstyle\mkern
