Lemma 6.14

(Forward Clause)
.

Let
G
be a good function and let
u
be an element of
\mathbb{A}^{\alpha}
.
Then, there is a good function
H\supseteq G
, such that
u
is in the domain of

H
.

Proof.

Let
F
be a witnessing function for
G
with domain
V
. Let

W:=V\cup\,{\sf supp}^{\alpha}(u)
given by
w_{0}\ll^{\alpha}\dots\ll^{\alpha}w_{m-1}
. We can effectively find rational numbers

q_{0}<\dots<q_{m-1}
such that, if
w_{j}\in V
, then
{\sf rank}_{0}(F(w_{j}))=q_{j}
. We set
F^{\star}(w_{j}):=F(w_{j})
, if

w_{j}\in V
and
F^{\star}(w_{j})={\sf X}_{q_{j}}
, otherwise.
Finally, we take

H:=G\cup\{{\langle u,{\sf ev}_{F^{\star}}(u)\rangle}\}
.
❑

Lemma 6.15

(Backwards Clause)
.

Let
G
be a good function and let
x
be an element of
\mathbb{A}
.
Then, there is a good function
H\supseteq G
, such that
x
is in the range of

H
.

Proof.

Let
F
witness that
G
is good and let
X
be the range of
F
.
By Theorem
6.12
, there is an independent set of
pseudo-variables
Y\supseteq X
, such that
x
is polynomially definable from
Y
.
Let
Y
be given by
y_{0}\ll\dots\ll y_{m-1}
. We can find rational numbers
q_{0}<\dots<q_{m-1}
, such that, if

y_{j}\in X
, then
F({\sf X}^{\alpha}_{q_{j}})=y_{j}
. We set
F^{\star}({\sf X}^{\alpha}_{q_{j}}):=y_{j}
. Let
u
be a code of the polynomial defining
x
from
W
employing the variables
{\sf X}_{q_{i}}
. Then, we can set
H=G\cup\{{\langle u,x\rangle}\}
.
The goodness of
H
is witnessed by
F^{\star}
.
❑

By standard arguments, we have the following lemma.

Lemma 6.16
.

Let
\varPhi
be given as the conjunction of
{\sf PA}^{-}
,
({{\sf PA}^{-}})^{\alpha}
, and the atomic, backwards and forwards clauses. Then,
\varPhi\vdash\varphi\leftrightarrow\varphi^{\alpha}
.

6.9.
Completing
{\sf PA}^{-}
plus Collection

We apply Corollary
3.4
with:

\bullet

\mathbb{A}
in the role of
\mathcal{M}
,

\bullet

\leqslant
in the role of
\sqsubset
,

\bullet

\mathbb{N}
in the role of

\mathcal{N}
,

\bullet

N
in the role of
\imath
,

\bullet

A
in the role of
\jmath
.

\bullet

\omega_{{\mathbb{A}}}^{\sf c}
in the role of
\theta
.

\bullet

The finite statement
\varPhi
(as in Lemma
6.16
) of the atomic and back and forth clauses
for the good functions together with
{\sf PA}^{-}
and
{{\sf PA}^{-}}^{\alpha}
delivers
our desired witness of a
{\sf INT}^{\sf fw}_{3}
-retract.

It is easy to see that our choices realise the conditions of the Corollary
3.4
.
We note that
\tau_{\sf N}
and
\omega_{{\mathbb{A}}}^{\sf c}
are
\Delta_{0}
.
So it follows that we have a sentence
\varPhi
, and a set
\mathcal{X}

of
\Delta_{1}
-formulas (over
\varPhi
), such
that
\varPhi+\mathcal{X}
axiomatises
\mathsf{Th}(\mathbb{A})
.

We conclude:

Theorem 6.17
.

The theory
{\sf PA}^{-}+{\sf Coll}
has a completion that is axiomatised by a sentence
\varPhi
plus a set of
\Sigma_{1}
-sentences.

6.10.
Free Riders

We note that, by a compactness argument, the ordered ring
\hat{\mathbb{A}}
is a Unique Factorisation Domain. Hence, our model
\mathbb{A}
of
{\sf PA}^{-}+{\sf Coll}
has additional salient properties, like the GCD property (existence of greatest common divisors). However, the GCD property is not provable in
{\sf PA}^{-}+{\sf Coll}
.
18
18

18

Indeed the GCD property is not even provable in
{\sf PA}^{-}+{\sf Coll}
since the iterated Shepherdson model constructed in the next section (which satisfies
\mathsf{Coll}
) inherits certain algebraic ‘defects’ of the Shepherdson model, including the failure of factorization into irreducibles.

To see this, let
\mathcal{R}
be the analogue of
\hat{\mathbb{A}}
, where we allow coefficients in
\mathbb{Q}
rather than in
\mathbb{Z}
, except for the case
of the coefficient with no variables. It is evident that
\mathcal{R}^{{\geqslant 0}}
is a model of
{\sf PA}^{-}
. Our method of verification of
\mathsf{Coll}
in
\mathbb{A}
can be used here as well to show that
\mathcal{R}^{{\geqslant 0}}
satisfies
\mathsf{Coll}
. However,
\mathcal{R}
is not even a factorization domain (let alone a unique factorization domain) and therefore it does not have the GCD property. This is based on the following observation: any
{\sf X}_{q}
can be written as the product of
(1/2){\sf X}_{q}
and
2
in
\mathcal{R}
, neither of which is a unit (invertible). Another way to see that the GCD property fails in
\mathcal{R}
is to note that the elements
{\sf X}_{0}
and
{\sf X}_{1}
do not have a greatest common divisor in
\mathcal{R}
.

7.
A restricted Completion of
IOpen
plus full Collection

In this section, we construct a recursive model, denoted
\mathbb{B}
, of
\mathsf{IOpen+Coll}
whose
complete theory is axiomatized by a collection of axioms consisting of a single
sentence together with an infinite number of
\Sigma_{1}
-sentences.
Intuitively speaking, the relationship of
\mathbb{B}
to
\mathbb{S}

(the Shepherdson model of
IOpen
) is that of
the model
\mathbb{A}
of the previous section to the model
\mathbb{Z}[{\sf X}]^{{\geqslant 0}}
. For this reason we sometimes refer to
\mathbb{B}
as the
\mathbb{Q}
-iterated Shepherdson model
.
19
19

19

As we shall see,
\mathbb{B}
has the ‘fractal property’ of being is isomorphic to many submodels of itself, some of which are proper initial segments of
\mathbb{B}.
Given that
{\sf IOpen}
is a much stronger theory than
{\sf PA}^{-}
, the model
\mathbb{B}
is far more complicated than
\mathbb{A}
.

7.1.
Basic properties of the
\mathbb{Q}
-iterated Shepherdson model

Assume that
\mathcal{R}
is an arbitrary discretely ordered commutative ring.
\mathsf{rc}(\mathcal{R})
denotes the real closure of the fraction field of
\mathcal{R}
. See Subsection
2.6
for the details of the construction. Note that we assume that
\mathcal{R}
is a subring of
{\sf rc}(\mathcal{R})
. Given an indeterminate
{\sf X}
(for our purposes
{\sf X}
will be of the form
{\sf X}_{q}
for some
q\in\mathbb{Q}
), the
Shepherdson extension of
\mathcal{R}
, denoted
\mathcal{S}(\mathcal{R},{\sf X})
is the ordered commutative subring of
\mathsf{rc}(\mathcal{R}[{\sf X}])
that consists of fractional polynomials of the following form, often referred to as Puiseux polynomials:

a_{n}{\sf X}^{n/q}+a_{n-1}{\sf X}^{n-1/q}+\ldots+a_{1}{\sf X}^{1/q}+a_{0},

where

1
.

n,q\in\mathbb{N}
,
q>0
.

2
.

a_{0}\in\mathcal{R}
; and

3
.

a_{i}\in\mathsf{rc}(\mathcal{R})
for
1\leqslant i\leqslant n.

The order is determined by making
{\sf X}
infinite relative to the elements of
\mathcal{R}
.
Thus,
\mathfrak{S}(\mathbb{Z},{\sf X})
is the Shepherdson model
\mathbb{S}
of
\mathsf{IOpen}
we considered in Section
5
.
We have the following basic lemma.

Lemma 7.1
.

Given a discretely ordered commutative ring
\mathcal{R}
, there is a unique isomorphism of
{\sf rc}(\mathfrak{S}(\mathcal{R},{\sf X}))
to
{\sf rc}(\mathcal{R}[{\sf X}])
over

\mathcal{R}[{\sf X}]
.

Proof.

This follows from Theorem
2.12
,
since
\mathcal{R}[{\sf X}]
is a subring of
\mathfrak{S}(\mathcal{R},{\sf X})
, and
\mathfrak{S}(\mathcal{R},{\sf X})
is a subring of
\mathsf{rc}(\mathcal{R}[{\sf X}])
.
❑

The following theorem is needed in the proof of Theorem
7.3
. Recall that a subring
\mathcal{R}
of an ordered field
\mathcal{K}
is
said to be an
integer part
of
\mathcal{K}
if for all
k\in\mathcal{K}
there is
r\in\mathcal{R}
such that
\left|r-k\right|<1
. Let us mention that
Shepherdson established the equivalence of (1) and (2). The equivalence of (2) and (3) follows from the well-known fact that if

\mathcal{R}
is an ordered subring of a real closed field
\mathcal{K}
, then there is a (unique) order preserving field embedding

e:\mathsf{rc}(\mathcal{R})\rightarrow\mathcal{K}
such that
e(x)=x
for all
x\in\mathcal{R}
.

Theorem 7.2

(Shepherdson
[
She64
]
)
.

The following are equivalent for a discrete ordered commutative ring
\mathcal{R}
:

1
.

\mathcal{R}^{{\geqslant 0}}\models{\sf IOpen}
.

2
.

\mathcal{R}
is an integer part of
\mathsf{rc}(\mathcal{R}).

3
.

\mathcal{R}
is an integer part of a real closed field.

Theorem 7.3

(Marker
[
Mar91
]
)
.

If
\mathcal{R}^{{\geqslant 0}}\models{\sf IOpen}
, then
\mathfrak{S}(\mathcal{R},{\sf X})^{{\geqslant 0}}\models{\sf IOpen}
.
20
20

20

Note that this theorem shows that every model of
\mathsf{IOpen}
(of any cardinality) has a proper end extension to a model of
\mathsf{IOpen}
.

Proof.

Let
\mathcal{R}
be a discrete ordered commutative ring such that
\mathcal{R}^{{\geqslant 0}}\models{\sf IOpen}
, and let
\mathcal{K}=\mathsf{rc}(\mathcal{R})
. Note that by Theorem
7.2

\mathcal{R}
is an integer part of
\mathcal{K}
. Let
\mathcal{K}^{*}
be the
Puiseux extension
of
\mathcal{K}
, i.e.,

\mathcal{K}^{*}:=\bigcup\limits_{n\in\mathbb{N}^{>0}}\mathcal{K}(\!({\sf X}^{1/n})\!),

where
{\sf X}
is infinite with respect to
\mathcal{K}
, and
\mathcal{K}(\!({\sf Y})\!)
is the field of formal Laurent series in the indeterminate
{\sf Y}
. Thus elements of
\mathcal{K}^{*}
are formal infinite series of the form

k^{*}=a_{n}{\sf X}^{n/q}+a_{n-1}{\sf X}^{n-1/q}+\ldots+a_{1}{\sf X}^{1/q}+a_{0}+a_{-1}{\sf X}^{-1/q}+a_{-2}{\sf X}^{-2/q}+\cdots

where each
a_{i}\in\mathcal{K}
and
n,q\in\mathbb{N}
, with
q>0
. It is well-known that
\mathcal{K}^{*}
is a real closed field (see, e.g., Robert Walker’s monograph
[
Wal78
]
).
21
21

21

Most sources (including
[
Wal78
]
) formulate this result as:
If
\mathcal{K}
is algebraically closed, then so is the Puiseux extension
\mathcal{K}^{*}
of
\mathcal{K}
.
As noted by Shepherdson
[
She64
]
, by putting together this formulation with the fact that an ordered field
\mathcal{F}
is real closed iff
\mathcal{F}[i]
is algebraically closed (where
i^{2}=-1
), one can readily verify that the Puiseux extension of a real closed field is real closed.

Given
k^{*}
as above, let

[k^{*}]=a_{n}{\sf X}^{n/q}+a_{n-1}{\sf X}^{n-1/q}+\ldots+a_{1}{\sf X}^{1/q}+[a_{0}],

where
[a_{0}]
is the unique element of
\mathcal{R}
of distance less than 1 from
a_{0}
such that
[a_{0}]\leqslant a_{0}
; note that
[a_{0}]
is well-defined since
\mathcal{R}
is an integer part of
\mathcal{K}
.
Observe that
[k^{*}]\in\mathfrak{S}(\mathcal{R},{\sf X})
and

0<k^{*}<[k^{*}]+1,

which makes it clear that
\mathfrak{S}(\mathcal{R},{\sf X})
is an integer part of
\mathcal{K}^{*}
. Thus by Theorem
7.2
,
\mathcal{R}^{{\geqslant 0}}\models{\sf IOpen}
.
❑

Recall from Section
6
that
\hat{\mathbb{A}}=\mathbb{Z}[{\sf X}_{q}\mathop{\mid}q\in\mathbb{Q}]
, and for
Y\in[\mathbb{Q}]^{<\omega}
,
\hat{\mathbb{A}}(Y)
is the subring of
\hat{\mathbb{A}}
generated by
\{{\sf X}_{q}\mathop{\mid}q\in Y\}
.

Definition 7.4
.

For
Y\in[\mathbb{Q}]^{<\omega}
, the ordered commutative ring
\hat{\mathbb{B}}(Y)
is defined as follows:

\bullet

\hat{\mathbb{B}}({\varnothing}):=\mathbb{Z}
,

\bullet

\hat{\mathbb{B}}({Y\cup\{q\}}):=\mathfrak{S}(\hat{\mathbb{B}}(Y),{\sf X}_{q})
, where
q>q^{\prime}
for each
q^{\prime}\in Y
.

❍

Lemma 7.5
.

Suppose
Y\in[\mathbb{Q}]^{<\omega}
and
q\in\mathbb{Q}
such that
q>q^{\prime}
for all
q^{\prime}\in Y
. Then we have:

\hat{\mathbb{B}}({Y\cup\{q\}})\cong\mathfrak{S}(\hat{\mathbb{B}}(Y),{\sf X}_{q}).

Moreover, the isomorphism is unique provided that it fixed each
{\sf X}_{q^{\prime}}
for all
q^{\prime}\in Y
.

Proof.

We prove this by an easy induction on the cardinality of
Y
using Lemma
7.1
.
❑

Corollary 7.6
.

For every
Y\in[\mathbb{Q}]^{<\omega}
, there is an order preserving ring embedding

f:\hat{\mathbb{B}}(Y)\hookrightarrow\mathsf{rc}\hat{(\mathbb{A})}
. Moreover, such an embedding is unique if it fixes each
{\sf X}_{q}
for
q\in Y
.

Remark 7.7
.

In light of Corollary
7.6
, from now on we
identify each ring
\hat{\mathbb{B}}(Y)
for
Y\in[\mathbb{Q}]^{<\omega}
with the corresponding
subring of
\mathsf{rc}\hat{(\mathbb{A})}
. Moreover, note that

q\in Y\textit{~iff~}{\sf X}_{q}\in\hat{\mathbb{B}}(Y).

Moreover, as noted in Remark
C.10
, for
Y,Y^{\prime}\in[\mathbb{Q}]^{<\omega}
, we have:

Y\subseteq Y^{\prime}\textit{~iff~}\hat{\mathbb{B}}(Y)\textit{~is~a~subring~of~}\hat{\mathbb{B}}(Y^{\prime}).

Definition 7.8
.

\mathbb{B}
(the
\mathbb{Q}
-iterated Shepherdson model) is defined as
\hat{\mathbb{B}}^{{\geqslant 0}}
, where

\hat{\mathbb{B}}:=\bigcup\limits_{Y\in[\mathbb{Q}]^{<\omega}}\hat{\mathbb{B}}(Y).

The
support
of an element
b\in\hat{\mathbb{B}}
, written
\mathsf{supp}(b)
, is the smallest

Y\in[\mathbb{Q}]^{<\omega}
(in the sense of inclusion) such that
b\in\hat{\mathbb{B}}(Y)
.
As shown in Appendix
C
,
\mathsf{supp}(b)
is well-defined.
❍

Corollary 7.9
.

There is an order preserving ring embedding
F:\hat{\mathbb{B}}\hookrightarrow\mathsf{rc}\hat{(\mathbb{A})}
. Moreover, such an embedding is unique if it fixes each
{\sf X}_{q}
for
q\in\mathbb{Q}
.

Proof.

Enumerate
\hat{\mathbb{B}}
as a countable sequence, and recursively construct
F
by successive applications of Corollary
7.6
.
❑

Remark 7.10
.

In light of Corollary
7.9
we shall identify
\hat{\mathbb{B}}
with the corresponding subring of
\mathsf{rc}\hat{(\mathbb{A})}
. Thus
\hat{\mathbb{A}}\subseteq\hat{\mathbb{B}}\subseteq\mathsf{rc}(\hat{\mathbb{A}})
, which makes it clear that
\mathsf{rc}\hat{(\mathbb{A})}=\mathsf{rc}\hat{(\mathbb{B})}.

Remark 7.11
.

Let
~\mathbb{B}(Y):=\hat{\mathbb{B}}(Y)^{{\geqslant 0}}
. Note that
\mathbb{B}=\bigcup\limits_{Y\in[\mathbb{Q}]^{<\omega}}\mathbb{B}(Y).

Coupled with Remark
7.7
, this implies that for
Y,Y^{\prime}\in[\mathbb{Q}]^{<\omega}
, we have:

Y\subseteq Y^{\prime}\textit{~iff~}\mathbb{B}(Y)\textit{~is~a~submodel~of~}\mathbb{B}(Y^{\prime}).

Lemma 7.12
.

\mathbb{B}
is the directed union of its constituent models
\mathbb{B}(Y)
,
as
Y
ranges in
[\mathbb{Q}]^{<\omega}.

Proof.

This is an immediate consequence of Remark
7.11
.
❑

Lemma 7.13
.

\mathbb{B}(Y)\models{\sf IOpen}
for every
Y\in[\mathbb{Q}]^{<\omega}.

Proof.

This follows from
|Y|
applications of Theorem
7.3
and Lemma
7.5
.
❑

Theorem 7.14
.

\mathbb{B}\models{\sf IOpen}
.

Proof.

This follows by putting Lemma
7.12
, and Lemma
7.13
together with the fact that
{\sf IOpen}
is
\forall\exists
-axiomatizable and
\forall\exists
-sentences are preserved under directed unions.
❑

Remark 7.15
.

Note that in light of
(
Shepherdson’s
)

Theorem
7.2
, Theorem
7.13
implies that
\hat{\mathbb{B}}(Y)
is an integer part of
\mathsf{rc}(\hat{\mathbb{A}}(Y))
for every

Y\in[\mathbb{Q}]^{<\omega}
, and Theorem
7.14
implies that
\hat{\mathbb{B}}
is an integer part of
\mathsf{rc}(\hat{\mathbb{A}})
.
This observation comes handy in the proof of Theorem
C.8
in the Appendix.

Definition 7.16
.

Recall (from Section
6
) that for
q\in\mathbb{Q}
,
\mathbb{Q}_{q}:=\{q^{\prime}\in\mathbb{Q}\mathop{\mid}q^{\prime}<q\}
. We put

\mathbb{B}_{q}:=\bigcup\limits_{Y\in[\mathbb{Q}_{q}]^{<\omega}}\mathbb{B}(Y).

❍

It can be readily checked that
\mathbb{B}_{q}\subsetneq_{\mathrm{end}}\mathbb{B}_{q^{\prime}}
whenever
q<q^{\prime}
. Indeed, as shown in Theorem
7.17
,
\mathbb{B}_{q}\prec_{\mathrm{end}}\mathbb{B}
for each
q\in\mathbb{Q}
, which makes it clear that
\mathbb{B}_{q}\prec_{\mathrm{end}}\mathbb{B}_{q^{\prime}}
for
q<q^{\prime}
.

Recall that
\mathfrak{S}(\mathcal{R},{\sf X})
is the Shepherdson extension of
\mathcal{R}
that adjoins an indeterminate
{\sf X}
to
\mathcal{R}
. Note that for
q<q^{\prime}
we have:

\mathbb{B}_{q}\subsetneq_{\mathrm{end}}\mathfrak{S}(\hat{\mathbb{B}}_{q},{\sf X}_{q})^{{\geqslant 0}}\subsetneq_{\mathrm{end}}\mathbb{B}_{q^{\prime}}.

We also observe that
\{{\sf X}_{q}^{n}\mathop{\mid}n\in\mathbb{N}\}
is cofinal in
\mathfrak{S}(\hat{\mathbb{B}}_{q},{\sf X}_{q})^{{\geqslant 0}}
, and

\{{\sf X}_{q}^{1/n}\mathop{\mid}n\in\mathbb{N}\}
is downward cofinal in
\mathfrak{S}(\hat{\mathbb{B}}_{q},{\sf X}_{q})^{{\geqslant 0}}\setminus\mathbb{B}_{q}.

Theorem 7.17
.

The model
\mathbb{B}_{q}
is a downwards closed cut of
\mathbb{B}
which is closed under the arithmetical
operations. Moreover,
\mathbb{B}_{q}\prec_{\mathrm{end}}\mathbb{B}
.

Proof.

The proof is similar to the proof of Theorem
6.4
, so it is left to the reader.
❑

Theorem 7.18
.

\mathbb{B}
satisfies
Coll
.

Proof.

In light of Remark
6.6
, this follows from Theorem
7.17
.
❑

Remark 7.19
.

The results of this section can be readily generalized as follows. Given any linearly ordered set
\mathbb{L}
one can define the
\mathbb{L}
-iterated Shepherdson model
\mathbb{S}_{\mathbb{L}}
by replacing
\mathbb{Q}
with
\mathbb{L}
in the definition of
\mathbb{B}
; thus
\mathbb{S}_{\mathbb{Q}}=\mathbb{B}
.
\mathbb{S}_{\mathbb{L}}
satisfies
\mathsf{IOpen}
for all linear orders
\mathbb{L}
. Moreover,
\mathbb{S}_{\mathbb{L}}
satisfies
\mathsf{Coll}
if
\mathbb{L}
has the property that for any
\ell^{\prime}\in\mathbb{L}
, there is some
\ell>_{\mathbb{L}}\ell^{\prime}
in
\mathbb{L}
such that there is an order isomorphism
f:\mathbb{L}_{\ell}\rightarrow\mathbb{L}
with the property that
f(x)=x
for all
x<_{\mathbb{L}}\ell^{\prime},
where
\mathbb{L}_{\ell}
is the initial segment of elements of
\mathbb{L}
that are dominated by
\ell
. Of course
\mathbb{S}_{\mathbb{L}}
also satisfies
\mathsf{Coll}
if
\mathbb{L}
is
\kappa
-like for some regular uncountable cardinal
\kappa
(i.e., the cardinality of
\mathbb{L}
is
\kappa
but the cardinality of every proper initial segment of
\mathbb{L}
is
\kappa
).

7.2.
Further analysis of
\mathbb{B}

Let
\mathbb{F}={\sf rc}(\hat{\mathbb{A}})
, and for each
q\in\mathbb{Q}
, let
\mathbb{F}_{q}:={\sf rc}(\hat{\mathbb{A}}_{q})
(where
\mathbb{\hat{A}}
and

\mathbb{\hat{A}}_{q}
are as in Section
6
). The definitions and results of the previous section make it clear that

\hat{\mathbb{B}}
can be described as the subring of
\mathbb{F}
consisting of all the finite sums of the form

a_{n}{\sf X}_{q_{n}}^{p_{n}}+\ldots+a_{1}{\sf X}_{q_{1}}^{p_{1}}+a_{0},

where

1
.

q_{i}\in\mathbb{Q}
and
p_{i}\in\mathbb{Q}^{{>0}}
, for
1\leqslant i\leqslant n;

2
.

a_{i}\in\mathbb{F}_{q_{i}}
for
1\leqslant i\leqslant n
;

3
.

a_{0}\in\mathbb{Z}
.

As before, the above representation is non-unique; for example, the expressions
{\sf X}_{0}^{2/4}+0\cdot{\sf X}_{0}^{1/4}
and
{\sf X}_{0}^{1/2}

represent the same element. To obtain a unique representation, we introduce the notion of
a standard form of an element of
\hat{\mathbb{B}}
to be an expression of form:

a_{n}{\sf X}_{q_{n}}^{p_{n}}+a_{n-1}{\sf X}^{p_{n-1}}_{q_{n-1}}+\ldots+a_{0},

where

1
.

q_{i}\in\mathbb{Q}
, and
p_{i}\in\mathbb{Q}^{{>0}}
, for
1\leqslant i\leqslant n
;

2
.

a_{i}\in\mathbb{F}_{q_{i}}
and
a_{i}\neq 0
, for
1\leqslant i\leqslant n
;

3
.

for
n\geqslant i>j\geqslant 1
, we have
q_{i}\geqslant q_{j}
and, if
q_{i}=q_{j}
, then
p_{i}>p_{j}
.

To see why every element of
\mathbb{B}
can be so presented, one simply ignores all the summands with coefficient
0
.

\bullet

A standard form expression
a_{n}{\sf X}_{q_{n}}^{p_{n}}+a_{n-1}{\sf X}^{p_{n-1}}_{q_{n-1}}+\ldots+a_{0}
is the
standard form for an element of
\mathbb{B}
if
a_{n}\geqslant 0.

The following definitions assume that
a\in\mathbb{B}
is written in standard form.

\bullet

{\sf rank}_{0}(a)=q_{n}
if
n>0
and
-\infty
otherwise.

\bullet

{\sf rank}(a)=(q_{n},p_{n})
, if
n>0
and
-\infty
otherwise.

Remark 7.20
.

Note that, for
x\in\mathbb{B}
,
{\sf rank}_{0}(x):=q
iff
q
is the least element of

\{q^{\prime}\in\mathbb{Q}\mathop{\mid}x\in\mathfrak{S}(\hat{\mathbb{B}}_{q^{\prime}},{\sf X}_{q^{\prime}})\}.

Thus if
{\sf rank}_{0}(x):=q
, then
x
can be written in the basic form
a{\sf X}_{q}^{p}+b
, where
p\in\mathbb{Q}^{{>0}}
,
a
is a positive element of
\hat{\mathbb{B}}_{q}
, and
b
is a Puiseux polynomial in
\mathfrak{S}(\hat{\mathbb{B}}_{q},{\sf X}_{q})
of degree less than
p
.

7.2.1.
Definable generators of
\mathbb{B}

The first two definitions are as in Section
6
:

\bullet

x\ll y
iff
\forall n\mathbin{\in}\omega\;(x+2)^{n}<y
.

\bullet

x\approx y
iff
\neg\,x\ll y\wedge\neg\,y\ll x
.

\bullet

{\sf m}(x)
iff
\forall b\,\bigl(0<b\ll x\rightarrow b\mathop{\mid}x\bigr)
.
Here
m
stands for ‘multiple’.

As in the case of the model
\mathbb{A}
of the previous section, we have:

Proposition 7.21
.

{\sf rank}_{0}(a)<{\sf rank}_{0}(b)
iff
a\ll b
.

We say that
a\in\mathbb{B}
is a
pseudo-variable
iff for some
q\in\mathbb{Q}
,
p\in\mathbb{Q}^{{>0}}
, and
b\in\mathbb{B}_{q}^{{>0}}
, we have that

b{\sf X}_{q}^{p}
is the standard form of
a
. Crucially, for our purposes, this property is definable in
\mathbb{B}
, as is shown in
the following theorem.

Theorem 7.22
.

For every
a\in\mathbb{B}
,
a
is a pseudo-variable iff both of the following hold
(
in
\mathbb{B}
)
:

1
.

{\sf m}(a)
;

2
.

\forall b,c\,\bigl((a=bc\wedge a\approx b\approx c)\rightarrow(2\mid b\wedge 2\mid c)\bigr).

Proof.

Fix
a
and assume that
a
is written in the standard form.
Assume first that
a
is a pseudo-variable, hence
a=a_{1}{\sf X}^{p}_{q}
for
a_{1}\in\mathbb{B}_{q}^{{>0}}
. We first show that

a
satisfies
(1)
. Suppose

0<b\ll a
. Then, both
b
and
a_{1}
are in
\mathbb{B}_{q}
. Since
b
is nonzero,

a_{1}b^{-1}{\sf X}_{q}^{p}\in\mathfrak{S}(\mathbb{B}_{q},{\sf X}_{q})\subseteq\mathbb{B}
.
So,
b
divides
a
.

We show that
a
satisfies
(2)
. We use the following convention: the last summmand in the standard form of an element
b\in\mathbb{B}
will be denoted
b^{*}
. Assume that
a=bc
and
a\approx b\approx c
. Aiming at a contradiction assume that
b
is odd, i.e., there is
d
such that
b=2d+1
(here we are using the fact that in
{\sf IOpen}
every number is odd or even, see
[
HP93
, Chapter I, Lemma 1.17]
). Note that this means that
b_{0}
(the unique summand in the standard form not multiplied by a variable) is odd and
b^{*}=b_{0}
. Since
a\approx b\approx c
and
a=bc
, it follows that
{\sf rank}(a)>{\sf rank}(b)
and
{\sf rank}(a)>{\sf rank}(c)
. By the definition of the standard form, the last summand in the standard form of
bc
is
c^{*}(b_{i}{\sf X}^{p_{i}}_{q_{i}}+\ldots+b_{1}{\sf X}^{p_{1}}_{q_{1}}+b_{0})
, where
i
is the greatest index in the standard form of
b
such that
{\sf rank}_{0}(c^{*})>q_{i}
(we allow
i=0
and hence
(bc)^{*}=b_{0}c^{*}
). However,
{\sf rank}(|(bc)^{*}|)={\sf rank}(|c^{*}|)\leqslant{\sf rank}(c)<(q,p).
Let
r
be such that
{\sf rank}(c^{*})<(q,r)<(q,p)
. This means that
a
is not divisible by
{\sf X}^{r}_{q}
- a contradiction.

Now assume
a
satisfies
(1)
and
(2)
. We observe that by 2.
a
must be nonstandard. Let
a_{n}{\sf X}_{q}^{p_{n}}+\ldots+a_{1}{\sf X}_{q}^{p_{1}}+a^{\prime}

be the standard form of
a
and
{\sf rank}_{0}(a^{\prime})<q
. If
a^{\prime}\neq 0
, then, for some
q^{\prime}<q
, the element
a
is not divisible by
{\sf X}_{q^{\prime}}
,
contradicting
{\sf m}(a)
. So,
a^{\prime}=0
. We claim that also
a_{n-1},\ldots,a_{1}
are all equal to
0
.
Assume not and let
i
be the least such that
a_{i}\neq 0
. Then

a=a_{n}{\sf X}_{q}^{p_{n}}+\ldots+a_{i}{\sf X}_{q}^{p_{i}}=|a_{i}|{\sf X}_{q}^{p_{i}}(a_{n}^{\prime}{\sf X}_{q}^{p_{n}^{\prime}}+\ldots+a_{i+1}^{\prime}{\sf X}_{q}^{p_{i+1}^{\prime}}+a_{i}/|a_{i}|),

where
p_{n}^{\prime},\ldots,p_{i+1}^{\prime}
are in all in
\mathbb{Q}^{{>0}}
. Note that
a_{i}/|a_{i}|\in\{1,-1\}
.
However,

a_{n}^{\prime}{\sf X}_{q}^{p_{n}^{\prime}}+\ldots+a_{i+1}{\sf X}_{q}^{p_{i+1}^{\prime}}+a_{i}/|a_{i}|

is odd,
contradicting that
a
satisfies
(2)
.
❑

Corollary 7.23
.

The Dorroh witnessing ideal
J
of
\mathbb{B}
is
\mathbb{B}
-definable without parameters.

Proof.

Put
J(x)
to be the formula “there is a sequence of pseudo-variables whose length is in
\omega
such that
x
is the sum of this sequence”.
❑

7.2.2.
Automorphisms

In this subsection, we develop the basics of automorphisms of
\mathbb{B}
.

Theorem 7.24
.

For any two sequences of pseudo-variables
x_{0}\ll x_{1}\ll\ldots\ll x_{\ell-1}
,

y_{0}\ll y_{1}\ll\ldots\ll y_{\ell-1}

there is an automorphism
\sigma
of
\mathbb{B}
which moves
x_{i}
to
y_{i}
. Moreover,
this automorphism extends to an automorphism of
\hat{\mathbb{B}}
;
indeed the automorphism can be extended to
\mathsf{rc}\hat{(\mathbb{B})}.

Proof.

Clearly, as in the proof of Theorem
6.9
, we can assume that
x_{i}={\sf X}_{q_{i}}
and

y_{i}=r_{i}{\sf X}_{q_{i}}^{p_{i}}
, where
q_{i}<q_{j}
if
i<j<\ell
.
We note that,
by the definition of
\mathbb{B}
,
the
r_{i}
are positive. Let
\{q^{\prime}_{0},\ldots,q^{\prime}_{k-1}\}=Y\subseteq\mathbb{Q}
be a finite set
such that all
r_{i}{\sf X}_{q_{i}}^{p_{i}}
are in
\mathbb{B}(Y)
, where

q^{\prime}_{i}<q^{\prime}_{j}
, for
i<j<k
. We note that the
q_{i}
will be in
Q
.
For
q^{\prime}\in Y
, we set:

\bullet

\nu({\sf X}_{q^{\prime}}):=r_{i}{\sf X}_{q^{\prime}}^{p_{i}}
if
q^{\prime}=q_{i}
for
i<\ell
,
and
\nu({\sf X}_{q^{\prime}}):={\sf X}_{q^{\prime}}
, otherwise.

For
k^{\prime}\leqslant k
,
let
Y_{k^{\prime}}=\{q^{\prime}_{0},\ldots,q^{\prime}_{k^{\prime}-1}\}
. By induction on
k^{\prime}\leqslant k
, we show that
there is a unique automorphism
\sigma_{q^{\prime}_{k^{\prime}}}
of
\mathbb{B}({Y_{k^{\prime}}})
which
extends
\nu
.

Assume that our claim holds for
k^{\prime}
and set
q^{\prime}=q^{\prime}_{k^{\prime}}
. Let
\sigma_{Y_{k^{\prime}}}
be the
automorphism of
\mathbb{B}({Y_{k^{\prime}}})

given by the inductive assumption. Let
\tau
be the unique extension of
\sigma_{Y_{k^{\prime}}}
to

{\sf rc}(\mathbb{B}(Y_{k^{\prime}}))
. Finally, we define
\sigma_{Y_{k^{\prime}+1}}
. Given an element
z
of the form

a_{n}{\sf X}_{q^{\prime}}^{n/m}+a_{n-1}{\sf X}_{q^{\prime}}^{(n-1)/m}+\ldots+a_{1}{\sf X}_{q^{\prime}}^{1/m}+a_{0},

put
\sigma_{Y_{k^{\prime}+1}}(z)
to be equal to

\tau(a_{n})\nu({\sf X}_{q^{\prime}})^{n/m}+\tau(a_{n-1})\nu({\sf X}_{q^{\prime}})^{(n-1)/m}+\ldots+\tau(a_{1})\nu({\sf X}_{q^{\prime}})^{1/m}+\tau(a_{0}).

The function we defined clearly preserves addition and multiplication.

We check that
\sigma_{Y_{n+1}}
is a bijection: we define its
inverse, say
\rho
. We first define the auxiliary function
\mu
. Let
q\in Y
.

\bullet

\mu({\sf X}_{q^{\prime}})=\tau^{-1}(r_{i})^{-\frac{1}{p_{i}}}{\sf X}_{q_{i}}^{\frac{1}{p_{i}}}
if
q^{\prime}=q_{i}
for
i<\ell
, and

\mu({\sf X}_{q^{\prime}})={\sf X}_{q^{\prime}}
, otherwise.

For an element
w
in the form

b_{n}{\sf X}_{q^{\prime}}^{n/m}+b_{n-1}{\sf X}_{q^{\prime}}^{(n-1)/m}+\ldots+b_{1}{\sf X}_{q^{\prime}}^{1/m}+b_{0},

we define
\rho(w)
to be

\tau^{-1}(b_{n})\mu({\sf X}_{q^{\prime}})^{n/m}+\tau^{-1}(b_{n-1})\mu({\sf X}_{q^{\prime}})^{(n-1)/m}+\ldots+\tau^{-1}(b_{1})\mu({\sf X}_{q^{\prime}})^{1/m}+\tau^{-1}(b_{0}),

We check that
\rho
is indeed the inverse of
\sigma_{Y_{n+1}}
, for the case that
q^{\prime}=q_{i}
. The other
case is simpler. Since
\sigma_{Y_{n+1}}
and
\rho
clearly commute with addition, it suffices
to do the computation for a single summand
a_{j}{\sf X}_{q_{i}}^{j/m}
or
b_{j}{\sf X}_{q_{i}}^{j/m}
.
We have:

\displaystyle\rho\sigma_{Y_{n+1}}(a_{j}{\sf X}_{q_{i}}^{j/m})

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\rho(\tau(a_{j})(r_{i}{\sf X}_{q_{i}}^{p_{i}})^{j/m})

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\tau^{-1}(\tau(a_{j})r_{i}^{j/m})((\tau^{-1}(r_{i})^{-\frac{1}{p_{i}}}{\sf X}_{q_{i}}^{\frac{1}{p_{i}}})^{p_{i}})^{j/m}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle a_{j}\tau^{-1}(r_{i}^{j/m})\tau^{-1}(r_{i})^{-j/m}X_{q_{i}}^{j/m}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle a_{j}{\sf X}^{j/m}_{q_{i}}

\displaystyle\sigma_{Y_{n+1}}\rho(b_{j}{\sf X}_{q_{i}}^{j/m})

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\sigma_{Y_{n+1}}(\tau^{-1}(b_{j})(\tau^{-1}(r_{i})^{-\frac{1}{p_{i}}}{\sf X}_{q_{i}}^{\frac{1}{p_{i}}})^{j/m})

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\tau(\tau^{-1}(b_{j})(\tau^{-1}(r_{i})^{-\frac{1}{p_{i}}})^{j/m})((r_{i}{\sf X}_{q_{i}}^{p_{i}})^{\frac{1}{p_{i}}})^{j/m}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle b_{j}(r_{i}^{-\frac{1}{p_{i}}})^{j/m}(r_{i}^{\frac{1}{p_{i}}})^{j/m}{\sf X}_{q_{i}}^{j/m}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle b_{j}{\sf X}_{q_{i}}^{j/m}

Clearly, the automorphism thus defined is unique.

Since for each
Y\in[\mathbb{Q}]^{<\omega}
, the function
\sigma_{Y}
is unique, we obtain that if
Y\in[\mathbb{Q}]^{<\omega}
contains all the
r_{i}{\sf X}_{i}^{q_{i}}
’s and

Y\subseteq Y^{\prime}\in[\mathbb{Q}]^{<\omega}
,
then
\sigma_{Y}\subseteq\sigma_{Y^{\prime}}
. Hence,
by the directedness of
\{\mathbb{B}(Y):\mathbb{Q}^{<\omega}\}
, we can define an automorphism
\sigma
of

\mathbb{B}
as the union of
\sigma_{Y}
’s, for all sufficiently large
Y
’s.

We prove that
\sigma
generates also an automorphism
\widetilde{\sigma}
of
\mathsf{rc}\hat{(\mathbb{B})}
. Recall from Subsection
7.2
that
\mathbb{F}:={\sf rc}(\hat{\mathbb{A}})
. Take any
\alpha\in\mathbb{F}
.
Then, there is
Y\in[\mathbb{Q}]^{<\omega}
,
such that
\alpha\in{\sf rc}(\hat{\mathbb{A}}(Y))
. Obviously
\alpha\in{\sf rc}(\hat{\mathbb{B}}({Y}))

and we know that there is a unique extension
\sigma_{1}
of
\sigma_{Y}
to
{\sf rc}(\hat{\mathbb{B}}(Y))
.
We put
\widetilde{\sigma}(\alpha)=\sigma_{1}(\alpha)
.
❑

7.3.
Interpreting
\mathbb{N}
in
\mathbb{B}

It suffices to show that
\hat{\mathbb{B}}
is a Dorroh ring, which is
readily seen from the definition of the elements of
\mathbb{B}
: if
J
is the ideal generated by all the elements of the form
\{{\sf X}_{q}^{p}:q\in\mathbb{Q},p\in\mathbb{Q}^{{>0}}\}
, then
\mathbb{B}/J
is isomorphic to
\mathbb{Z}.
We let
\omega(x)
be the
\Delta_{0}
arithmetical formula defining
\mathbb{N}
in
\mathbb{B}
. We call the resulting interpretation
N
.

7.4.
Interpreting
\mathbb{B}
in
\mathbb{N}

We use the coding from Section
2.6
to represent arithmetically the structure of
\mathbb{B}
: an element of
\mathbb{B}

is represented via its standard form as a sequence of triples

\{\langle a_{i},q_{i},p_{i}\rangle\}_{i\leqslant n}
where, for each
i
,

a_{i}\in\mathbb{F}_{q_{i}}
,

q_{i}
’s are rational numbers (representing the indices of variables) and
p_{i}
’s are non-negative rational numbers (representing the exponents; we assume that
q_{0}=0
). We note that
a_{i}
’s are represented as in Section
2.6
, so each
a_{i}
is a pair
(p,l)
, where
p
is a polynomial with coefficients in
\mathbb{Z}[{\sf X}_{q}:q<q_{i}](=\hat{\mathbb{A}}_{q_{i}})
. We assume that the sequences of
a_{i}
’s,
q_{i}
’s and
p_{i}
’s meet the requirements defining the standard form of an element of
\mathbb{B}
.

We shall often view the sequence of triples
\{\langle a_{i},q_{i},p_{i}\rangle\}_{i\leqslant n}
as the triple of sequences
\langle\bar{a},\bar{q},\bar{p}\rangle
. For
q\in\mathbb{Q}
, let

\sim_{q}
abbreviate

\sim_{\mathbb{Z}[\{{\sf X}_{r}:r<q\}]},

and let

\sim
abbreviate

\sim_{\mathbb{Z}[\{{\sf X}_{r}:r\in\mathbb{Q}\}]},

where for a ring
\mathcal{R}
,
\sim_{\mathcal{R}}
is defined in Section
2.6
. We say that sequences
s=\langle\bar{a}_{1},\bar{p}_{1},\bar{q}_{1}\rangle
, and
t=\langle\bar{a}_{2},\bar{p}_{2},\bar{q}_{2}\rangle
represent the same element of
\mathbb{B}
iff
{\sf length}(s)={\sf length}(t)
and for each
i<{\sf length}(s)
,
\bar{a}_{1}(i)\sim\bar{a}_{2}(i)
,
\bar{p}_{1}(i)=\bar{p}_{2}(i)
,
\bar{q}_{1}(i)=\bar{q}_{2}(i)
. By the results of Section
2.6
such a presentation gives rise to a recursive ring, which is isomorphic to
\mathbb{B}
.

As previously, we will use
\mathbb{B}^{\alpha}
to denote that
\mathbb{N}
-internal representation of
\mathbb{B}
.
We call the resulting interpretation
B
.

7.5.
Back-and-forth in
\mathbb{B}

We start by defining the support function between
elements of
\mathbb{B}
and finite sets of variables. It is shown in Appendix
C
(see Theorem
C.9
and the definition preceding it) that the support of an element
b\in\mathbb{B}
(defined right after Definition
7.8
) coincides with the smallest set of variables
\mathfrak{X}
such that
b\in{\sf rc}(\mathbb{Z}[\mathfrak{X}])
.

Suppose that
b=a_{n}{\sf X}_{q_{n}}^{p_{n}}+a_{n-1}{\sf X}_{q_{n-1}}^{p_{n-1}}+\ldots+a_{0}
is the standard form of an element from
\mathbb{B}
. A set
\mathfrak{X}
is the support of
b
iff
\mathfrak{X}
is the least such that for all
i
,
{\sf X}_{q_{i}}\in\mathfrak{X}
and each
a_{i}
is algebraic over
\hat{\mathbb{A}}(\mathfrak{X})
. Since each element
b\in\mathbb{B}^{\alpha}
is a polynomial, whose coefficients are given by some concrete notations, we shall take
{\sf supp}^{\alpha}(b)
to be the set of variables that occur in
b
or the notations for coefficients of
b
. This is clearly not invariant under the equality relation on
\mathbb{B}^{\alpha}
, but this is not problematic for our purposes.

Let us now define the appropriate variants of the
ev
and
app
functions from Section
5.3
. This is needed, since now we need to represent not only the real algebraic numbers, but also the elements of
{\sf rc}(\mathbb{Z}[\{{\sf X}_{q}:q\in\mathbb{Q}\}]).
To this we will need:

\bullet

a code of a notation
(f,l)
as in the Section
2.6
for
\mathcal{R}=\mathbb{Z}[{\sf X}_{q}:q\in\mathbb{Q}]
, where
f
is a polynomial
with coefficients in

\mathbb{Z}[{\sf X}_{q}:q\in\mathbb{Q}]
. We shall represent
f
as a sequence

(f_{1},\ldots,f_{n})
, where each
f_{i}\in\mathbb{Z}[{\sf X}_{q}:q\in\mathbb{Q}]
.

\bullet

a finite tuple of variables
\bar{{\sf X}}:={\langle{\sf X}_{q_{0}},\ldots,{\sf X}_{q_{k-1}}\rangle}
,
where
k
is the number of variables occurring in the
f_{i}
’s;

\bullet

a variable
{\sf X}_{q}
, where
q>q_{i}
.

Having the above fixed, we can write the formula

Q_{\bar{{\sf X}},f,x}({\sf Y}):=f_{n}[\bar{{\sf X}}]{\sf Y}^{n}+f_{n-1}[\bar{{\sf X}}]{\sf Y}^{n-1}x+\ldots+f_{1}[\bar{{\sf X}}]{\sf Y}x^{n-1}+f_{0}[\bar{{\sf X}}]x^{n},

where
f_{i}[\bar{{\sf X}}]
results from
f_{i}
by replacing the
i
-th variable with
{\sf X}_{q_{i}}
. The point of
Q_{\bar{{\sf X}},f,x}({\sf Y})
is that
a
is a root of
f[\bar{{\sf X}}]
iff
a{\sf X}_{q}^{p}
is a root of
Q_{\bar{{\sf X}},f,{\sf X}_{q}^{p}}({\sf Y})
in
{\sf Y}
(assuming the sanity condition that
q
is greater than all the indices in
\bar{{\sf X}}
). As in Section
5.3
, for a given
p
, we can actually turn this into parameter-free
\mathbb{B}
-definable function

{\sf app}^{*}_{x,s}(f,y)=Q_{s,f,x}(y)
.
Now given the notation
(f,k)
, sequence
s
of length
k
and arbitrary
x

\bullet

{\sf ev}_{x,s}((f,k))=z
iff there is an
s^{\prime}\in{\sf seq}
such that:

\circ

{\sf length}(s^{\prime})=k
,

\circ

\pi(s^{\prime},k-1)=z
,

\circ

for all
i<k
, we have
{\sf app}^{*}_{x,s}(f,\pi(s^{\prime},i))=0
,

\circ

for all
i<j<k
, we have
\pi(s^{\prime},i)<\pi(s^{\prime},j)
,

\circ

if
y\mathbin{\leqslant}z
and

{\sf app}^{*}_{x,s}(f,y)=0
, then, for some
j<k
, we have
\pi(s^{\prime},j)=y
.

With these definitions, we can reprove a version of the extension lemma. We say that an element
a\in\mathbb{B}
is
real-algebraic
definable from a set
A
, if for some polynomial
f\in\mathbb{Z}[A][{\sf Y}]
and some
k\in\mathbb{N}
,
a
is the
k
-th root of
f
.

Lemma 7.25
.

Let
W
be a finite, independent set of pseudo-variables. Then, there is a finite set of variables
V
such
that
W\cup V
is independent and
{\sf supp}(W)
is
(
pointwise
)

real-algebraic definable from
W\cup V
.

Proof.

We borrow the notation from the proof of
Lemma
6.11
and let
W^{*}
,
V
and
Z
be defined as
before.
As previously, it suffices to show that the elements in
W^{*}
are real-algebraic definable from
Z
. Let

{\sf X}_{q_{0}},\ldots,{\sf X}_{q_{k-1}}
be all such elements. We use induction on
i\leqslant k-1
.

Suppose the claim holds for all
j<i
. Note that the
i
-th element of
W
must be of the
form
a{\sf X}_{q_{i}}^{p}
. We know that
a\in{\sf rc}(\mathbb{Z}[\{{\sf X}_{r}\in{\sf supp}(W):r<q_{i}\}])
,
so there is a notation
(f,l)
and a sequence of variables
\bar{{\sf X}}\in{\sf supp}(W)
with
indices less than
q_{i}
such that
{\sf ev}_{{\sf X}_{q_{i}}^{p},\bar{{\sf X}}}((f,l))=a{\sf X}_{q_{i}}^{p}

and
{\sf X}_{q_{i}}
is the unique element
y
for which
{\sf ev}_{{\sf X}_{q_{i}}^{p},\bar{{\sf X}}}((f,l))=a{\sf X}_{q_{i}}^{p}
.
Unraveling the definition of
{\sf ev}
this means that
{\sf X}_{q_{i}}^{p}
is a root of

f_{n}[\bar{{\sf X}}](a{\sf X}_{q_{i}}^{p})^{n}+f_{n-1}[\bar{{\sf X}}](a{\sf X}_{q_{i}}^{p})^{n-1}{\sf Y}+\ldots+f_{1}[\bar{{\sf X}}](a{\sf X}_{q_{i}}^{p}){\sf Y}^{n-1}+f_{0}[\bar{{\sf X}}]{\sf Y}^{n},

seen as a polynomial in
{\sf Y}
. In particular,
{\sf X}_{q_{i}}
is real algebraic definable over
Z\cup\bar{{\sf X}}
.
By inductive assumption
\bar{{\sf X}}
is real-algebraic definable over
Z
, which concludes our proof.
❑

We shall keep the definition of a witnessing function unchanged, of course given the current meaning of “being a pseudo-variable”.
We observe that given a representation
u\in\mathbb{B}^{\alpha}
and a witnessing function
F
, we can uniformly define an element
{\sf val}_{F}(u)
. We note that the definition of
{\sf val}_{F}
makes use of the
{\sf ev}
function. We say that a partial function
G:\mathbb{B}^{\alpha}\rightarrow\mathbb{B}
is good is there is a witnessing function
F
such that for each
u\in{\sf dom}(G)
,
G(u)={\sf val}_{F}(u).

Theorem 7.26
.

Suppose
W
is an independent set of pseudo-variables and
x
is any element of
\mathbb{B}
. Then, there is a witnessing function
F
such that
{\sf rg}(F)\supseteq W
and for some
u\in\mathbb{B}^{\alpha}
,
x={\sf val}_{F}(u)
.

Proof.

Let
W
and
x
be as in the assumption. Let
\mathfrak{X}
support
x
and let

\mathfrak{X}^{\prime}=\mathfrak{X}\setminus\{{\sf X}_{q}:a{\sf X}_{q}^{p}\in W\}.

Let
V
be as promised in Lemma
7.25
for
W
.
We take
U:=W\cup V\cup\mathfrak{X}^{\prime}
. Since
V
consists of variables,

U
is independent. Since
{\sf supp}(W)
is real-algebraic definable from
W\cup V
,
it follows that every element of
\mathfrak{X}
is real algebraic definable from
U
. In particular, every coefficient of the standard form of
x
is in the real closure of
\mathbb{Z}[U]
. Hence we can fix a sequence of notations
\{(f_{i},n_{i})\}_{i\leqslant k}
, rational numbers
\{p_{i}\}_{i\leqslant k},\{q_{i}\}_{i\leqslant k}
such that by putting
u={\langle\{(f_{i},n_{i})\}_{i\leqslant k},\{q_{i}\}_{i\leqslant k},\{p_{i}\}_{i\leqslant k}\rangle}
we have
x={\sf val}_{F}(u),
where
F
interprets the variables of
f_{i}
’s as the appropriate elements from
U
.
❑

With these definitions the Atomic, Backward and Forward Clauses of the Ehrenfeucht-Fraïssé game can be proved exactly as in Section
6.8
.

The following question is prompted by the fact that
\mathbb{B}
is a recursive model of
\mathsf{IOpen}
; see also Question
5.3
.

Open Question 7.27
.

Is there a model of
\mathsf{IOpen+Coll}
whose complete theory has a restricted axiomatization, and which satisfies normality and other desirable algebraic properties that are known to hold in some recursive (computable) nonstandard model of
\mathsf{IOpen}
?
❍

7.6.
Completing
IOpen
plus Collection

We apply Corollary
3.4
with:

\bullet

\mathbb{B}
in the role of
\mathcal{M}
,

\bullet

\leqslant
in the role of
\sqsubset
,

\bullet

\mathbb{N}
in the role of

\mathcal{N}
,

\bullet

N
in the role of
\imath
,

\bullet

B
in the role of
\jmath
.

\bullet

\omega_{{\mathbb{B}}}^{\sf c}
in the role of
\theta
.

\bullet

The finite statement of the atomic and back and forth clauses
for the good functions together with
{\sf PA}^{-}
and
{{\sf PA}^{-}}^{\alpha}
delivers
our desired witness of a
{\sf INT}^{\sf fw}_{3}
-retract.

It is easy to see that our choices realise the conditions of the Corollary
3.4
.
We note that
\tau_{\sf N}
and
\omega_{{\mathbb{B}}}^{\sf c}
are
\Delta_{0}
.
So, it follows that we have a sentence
\varPhi
, and a set
\mathcal{X}

of
\Delta_{1}
-formulas
X
(over
\varPhi
), such
that
\varPhi+\mathcal{X}
axiomatises
\mathsf{Th}(\mathbb{B})
.

We conclude:

Theorem 7.28
.

The theory
{\sf IOpen}+{\sf Coll}
has a completion that is axiomatised by a sentence
\varPhi
plus a set of
\Sigma_{1}
-sentences.

7.7.
Free Riders

As in the case of the Shepherdson model
\mathbb{S}
, there seem to be no free riders since the iterated Shepherdson model inherits the algebraic defects of
\mathbb{S}
.

8.
A Final Word

In this section we first collect the open questions that arose throughout the paper, and then we briefly describe the shape and content of our projected sequel(s) of this work.

8.1.
Open Problems

Open Question 8.1
.

Is there a consistent completion of
\mathrm{I}\Delta_{0}
that is of restricted complexity?

As indicated in the discussion following Question
1.2
(in the introduction), new ideas and machinery seem to be needed to resolve the above question.
❍

Open Question 8.2
.

Are there examples that show that the class
\mathcal{T}_{\mathrm{nrestr}}
of arithmetical theories extending
\mathsf{Q}
that
do not have a restricted completion can be separated from the classes
\mathcal{T}_{\mathrm{MRDP}}
,
\mathcal{T}_{\mathrm{truth}}
, and

\mathcal{T}_{\mathrm{nrec}}
(defined at the end of the introduction)?
❍

Open Question 8.3
.

Is there yet another way to construct a witnessing sentence for an
{\sf INT}_{3}^{\sf fw}
-Retract?

The above question was posed as Question
3.5
. See subsection
3.3
for the motivation of this question.
❍

Open Question 8.4
.

Characaterize the authomorphism group
\mathrm{Aut}(\mathbb{A})
of
\mathbb{A}
(a similar question can be asked about

\mathrm{Aut}(\mathbb{B})
).

The above question below was posed as Question
6.10
. Note that
\mathrm{Aut}(\mathbb{A})
contains a
copy of the automorphism group
\mathrm{Aut}({\mathbb{Q}})
of the linear order
\mathbb{Q}
, and therefore it is of size continuum.
❍

Open Question 8.5
.

Is there a model of
\mathsf{IOpen+Coll}
whose complete theory has a restricted axiomatization and which satisfies normality and other desirable algebraic properties that are known to hold in some recursive (computable) nonstandard model of
\mathsf{IOpen}
?

The above question was posed as
Question
7.27
. We suspect that the answer is in the positive.
❍

8.2.
Future Work

Our work arose from the quest for a more balanced and nuanced understanding of the manifestations of incompleteness
in the form of
restricted complexity of the completions
, as in
[
EV24
]
. In contrast with the focus of this paper on weak arithmetical theories based on the
\Sigma_{n}
-hierarchy, in future work we aim to pursue the program of employing
new measures
of complexity
to probe stronger theories.

For example, let
{\sf PA}_{n}
denotes the set of theorems of
{\sf PA}
(Peano Arithmetic) of complexity at most
\Pi_{n}
, and consider the
\Delta_{0}(\Sigma_{n})
hierarchy of arithmetical formulae, i.e., formulae that are
\Delta_{0}
relative to some
\Sigma_{n}
-definable oracle. We have been able to establish the following result.

Theorem 8.6
.

For every sufficiently large
n
there is a
k
such that,
{\sf PA}_{n}
has a completion which is axiomatizable by a single sentence and an infinite set of
\Delta_{0}(\Sigma_{k})
-sentences.

Note that in contrast to
IOpen
, no
{\mathsf{I}}\Sigma_{n}
with nonzero
n
can be axiomatized with a set of
\Sigma_{n}
formulae since as shown in
[
EV24
]
no consistent completion of
\mathrm{I}\Delta_{0}+\mathsf{Exp}
is of restricted complexity. We conjecture that actually
{\sf PA}_{n+2}
admits a completion which is axiomatizable by a single sentence and a set of
\Delta_{0}(\Sigma_{n+1})
sentences.

We have also obtained an analogue of Theorem
8.6
for
\mathsf{ZF}_{n}
(the
\Pi_{n}
-consequences of
\mathsf{ZF}
-set theory).

In our future work we also aim to apply similar ideas to explore incompleteness phenomena for
second order theories
of arithmetic and set theory.

Appendix A
Proof of Theorem
5.1

We show the following in the Shepherdson model
\mathbb{S}
:
if
\alpha
is irreducible in
\mathbb{S}
, then
\alpha\in\mathbb{N}
.

We write
{\sf sg}(a):=a/|a|
, in case
a\neq 0
. In other words,
{\sf sg}(a)=1
, if
a>0
, and

{\sf sg}(a)=-1
if
a<0
.

Proof.

Suppose
\alpha
is non-standard. Say
\alpha=a_{n}{\sf X}^{n/q}+\ldots+a_{0}
, where
n>0
.
We show that
\alpha
has a non-trivial factorisation.

We first consider the case that
a_{0}=0
. Suppose
a_{0}=\ldots=a_{m}=0
, where
m<n
.
Then,

\alpha={\sf X}^{1/(2q)}(a_{n}{\sf X}^{(2n-1)/(2q)}+\ldots+a_{m+1}{\sf X}^{(2m+1)/(2q)}).

We treat the case that
a_{0}\neq 0
. We can safely assume that
a_{0}=\pm 1
, since otherwise
we can find a prime
p
that divides
a_{0}
, which allows us to factor
\alpha
as a
product of two nonunit elements of
\mathbb{S}
by writing
p(\frac{1}{p}\alpha)
.
Consider
\beta:=a_{n}{\sf X}^{3n}+a_{n-1}{\sf X}^{3(n-1)}+\ldots+a_{0}
.

Suppose
\beta=0
has a real solution, say
b
. Then,

\beta=({\sf X}-b)(c_{n-1}{\sf X}^{3n-1}+c_{n-2}{\sf X}^{3n-4}\ldots+c_{0}).

We note that
-bc_{0}=a_{0}
and
c_{n-1}=a_{n}>0
. We find:

\alpha=(|c_{0}|{\sf X}^{1/(3q)}+{\sf sg}(c_{0})a_{0})((c_{n-1}/|c_{0}|){\sf X}^{(3n-1)/(3q)}+\ldots+{\sf sg}(c_{0})).

Clearly, both components are in
\mathbb{S}
and unequal to
1
.

Suppose
\beta=0
has a complex, non-real solution, say
{\mathfrak{d}}
. Then,
\overline{{\mathfrak{d}}}
, the conjugate of
{\mathfrak{d}}
, is also
a solution which is unequal to
{\mathfrak{d}}
. Let
({\sf X}-{\mathfrak{d}})({\sf X}-\overline{{\mathfrak{d}}})
be
{\sf X}^{2}+d_{1}{\sf X}+d_{0}
, where the
d_{i}
are real.
Then,

\beta=({\sf X}^{2}+d_{1}{\sf X}+d_{0})(c_{n-2}{\sf X}^{3n-2}+c_{n-3}{\sf X}^{3n-5}\ldots+c_{0}).

We note that
d_{0}c_{0}=a_{0}
and
c_{n-2}=a_{n}>0
. We find:

\alpha=(|c_{0}|{\sf X}^{2/(3q)}+|c_{0}|d_{1}{\sf X}^{1/(3q)}+{\sf sg}(c_{0})a_{0})((c_{n-2}/|c_{0}|){\sf X}^{(3n-2)/(3q)}+\ldots+{\sf sg}(c_{0})).

Clearly, both components are in
\mathbb{S}
and unequal to
1
.
❑

Remark A.1
.

Of course, in most cases our decomposition is very inefficient.
For, example, if
a_{0}>1
, we have the simple decomposition

\alpha=a_{0}((a_{n}/a_{0}){\sf X}^{n/q}+\ldots+1)
. If

a_{0}<-1
, we have
\alpha=-a_{0}((-a_{n}/a_{0}){\sf X}^{n/q}+\ldots+({-}1))
.

Appendix B
Where does Collection get its Bite?

In this appendix, we give a sharp proof of the well-known insight that collection implies
induction over a sufficiently strong arithmetical base theory. Specifically, we show that
we can choose this base theory to be
\mathrm{I}\mathsf{E}_{1}
, to wit the theory of bounded existential induction.
We have:

Theorem B.1
.

\mathrm{I}\mathsf{E}_{1}+\forall_{1}\mbox{-}{\sf Coll}\vdash\mathrm{I}\exists_{1}
.

The proof is a direct adaptation of the proof of
[
Kay91
, Lemma 7.4, p84]

Theorem B.2
.

\mathrm{I}\mathsf{E}_{1}+{\sf Coll}\vdash{\sf PA}
.

Proof.

By the results in
[
Kay90
]
, we know that
\mathrm{I}\mathsf{\exists}_{1}

extends
EA
, so:

\displaystyle\mathrm{I}\mathsf{E}_{1}+{\sf Coll}

\displaystyle\vdash

\displaystyle\mathrm{I}\exists_{1}+{\sf Coll}

\displaystyle\vdash

\displaystyle{\sf EA}+{\sf Coll}

\displaystyle\vdash

\displaystyle{\sf PA}

❑

Appendix C
The support function

Recall that
\hat{\mathbb{A}}:=\mathbb{Z}[\,\mathsf{X}_{q}\mathop{\mid}q\in\mathbb{Q]}
, and
\hat{\mathbb{B}}
is the
\mathbb{Q}
-iterated Sheperdson model, as
defined in Sections
6
and
7
(respectively). In this subsection we develop the basic properties of the support function applied to the rings
\hat{\mathbb{A}}
and
\hat{\mathbb{B}}
. This is straightforward for the case of

\hat{\mathbb{A}}
, but the case of
\hat{\mathbb{B}}
takes more work,
which is to be expected, since
\hat{\mathbb{B}}
is a far more complicated
structure. As we shall see, it will be useful to take advantage of an appropriate support
function for the field
\mathbb{F}:=\mathsf{rc}(\hat{\mathbb{A}})=\mathsf{rc}(\hat{\mathbb{B}})
in order to develop the basic properties of
the support function in the ring
\hat{\mathbb{B}}.

Given a linear order
\mathbb{L}
, all of the results in this section
readily generalize to the ring
\mathbb{Z}[\,\mathsf{X}_{\ell}\mathop{\mid}\ell\in\mathbb{L]}
, and to the
\mathbb{L}
-iterated Shepherdson model
\mathbb{S}_{\mathbb{L}}
,
as here we only rely on the fact that
\mathbb{Q}
is a linear order.

C.1.
The support function in
\hat{\mathbb{A}}

By definition, every element
a\in\hat{\mathbb{A}}
either can be be put in the form
m

for some
m\in\mathbb{Z}
(in which case
a
is referred to as a
constant polynomial
), or in the form
m+\pi_{1}+\cdot\cdot\cdot+\pi_{n}
, where
m\in\mathbb{Z},
and each
\pi_{i}
is a
neat monomial
,
i.e., is a product of the form
b\mathsf{X}_{q_{1}}^{m_{1}}...\mathsf{X}_{q_{k}}^{m_{k}}
, where
b\in\mathbb{Z}
is nonzero,
q_{i}\in\mathbb{Q}
, with
q_{1}>\cdot\cdot\cdot>q_{n}
, and
k
and each
m_{i}
are nonzero natural numbers.
Let us refer to such a representation of a nonconstant polynomial as a
neat

monomial representation
.

Lemma C.1
.

Any two neat monomial representations of the same element of

\hat{\mathbb{A}}
are equal up to the summing order of the neat monomials.

Proof.

If
e
and
e^{\prime}
are neat monomial representations of the same element in
\hat{\mathbb{A}}
, then

(*)

e-e^{\prime}=0
holds in
\hat{\mathbb{A}}.

If
e^{\prime}
is not obtainable from
e
by changing the summing order
of the neat monomials of
e
, then the set of neat monomials that occur in
e
does not
coincide with the set of neat monomials that occur in
e^{\prime}
. This shows
that the left-hand side of the equation
(*)
is a nonzero polynomial, thus
contradicting
(*)
.
❑

The above lemma assures us that the definition below yields a well-defined function
\mathsf{supp}:\hat{\mathbb{A}}\rightarrow[\mathbb{Q}]^{<\omega}
. We often refer to
\mathsf{supp}(a)
as ‘the support of
a
’.

Definition C.1
.

Given
a\in\hat{\mathbb{A}},

\mathsf{supp}(a)=\varnothing
if
a
is a constant polynomial, and otherwise
\mathsf{supp}(a)
is the set of
q\in\mathbb{Q}
such that
\mathsf{X}_{q}
occurs
in a neat monomial representation of
a
.
❍

Recall that for
Y\in[\mathbb{Q}]^{<\omega},

\hat{\mathbb{A}}(Y):=\mathbb{Z}[\,\mathsf{X}_{q}\mathop{\mid}q\in Y\mathbb{]}.
The following characterization
of the support function can be readily established using the definitions
involved.

Theorem C.2
.

The following statements are equivalent for
a\in\hat{\mathbb{A}}
, and
Y\in[\mathbb{Q}]^{<\omega}
.

1
.

\mathsf{supp}(a)=Y.

2
.

Y
is a minimal element of
[\mathbb{Q}]^{<\omega}
such that
a\in\hat{\mathbb{A}}(Y)
.

3
.

Y
is the minimum element of
[\mathbb{Q}]^{<\omega}
such that
a\in\hat{\mathbb{A}}(Y)
.

C.2.
The support function in
\hat{\mathbb{B}}

In order to develop the support function for
\hat{\mathbb{B}},
we recall
some basic facts from field theory that allow us to get hold of an appropriate support
function for the field
\mathbb{F}:=\mathsf{rc}(\hat{\mathbb{A}})=\mathsf{rc}(\hat{\mathbb{B}})
.

Given any field
\mathcal{K}
,
\mathsf{acl}_{\mathcal{K}}:\mathcal{P}(\mathcal{K})\rightarrow\mathcal{P}(\mathcal{K})
is the relative-to-
\mathcal{K}
algebraic closure function defined as follows: given
S\subseteq\mathcal{K}
,
\mathsf{acl}_{\mathcal{K}}(S)=\{a\in\mathcal{K}:

a
is
algebraic over
S\}
, where
a
is said to be algebraic over
S
, if there
are
b_{1},\cdot\cdot\cdot,b_{n}
in
S
, and some nonzero polynomial
p(\mathsf{X}_{1},\cdot\cdot\cdot,\mathsf{X}_{n},\mathsf{X}_{n+1})\in\mathbb{Q}[\mathsf{X}_{1},\cdot\cdot\cdot,\mathsf{X}_{n},\mathsf{X}_{n+1}]
such that
p(b_{1},\cdot\cdot\cdot,b_{n},a)=0
holds in
\mathcal{K}
. It is a basic fact of field theory that
\mathsf{acl}_{\mathcal{K}}(S)
is a subfield of
\mathcal{K}
for all
S\subseteq\mathcal{K}
.

Under the above definition,
\mathsf{acl}_{\mathcal{K}}
satisfies the
properties given in Definition (
C.3
) when
\mathsf{cl}
is interpreted as
\mathsf{acl}_{\mathcal{K}}
, known as the axioms of
pregeometry
22
22

22

Pregeometries are also referred to as ‘finitely based matroids’ in the literature. We found the chapter on Geometric Model Theory in the lecture notes by Manuel Bodirsky to be useful in the preparation of this section; see:

https://wwwpub.zih.tu-dresden.de/
\sim
bodirsky/Model-theory.pdf
. The idempotence axiom in this context is often paraphrased by algebraists as the principle:
algebraic over algebraic is algebraic
; and the exchange axiom is commonly known as the
Steinitz Exchange Lemma
, which is a basic result in field theory. The rest of the axioms are simple consequences of the definitions involved.

Definition C.3
.

Suppose
X
is a set, and
\mathsf{cl}:\mathcal{P}(X)\rightarrow\mathcal{P}(X)
.
(X,\mathsf{cl})
is pregeometry if the following statements hold for all subsets
S
and
S^{\prime}
of
X
:

\bullet

Expansion:
S\subseteq\mathsf{cl}(S).

\bullet

Idempotence:
\mathsf{cl}(\mathsf{cl}(S))=\mathsf{cl}(S).

\bullet

Monotonicity:
S\subseteq S^{\prime}\Rightarrow\mathsf{cl}(S)\subseteq\mathsf{cl}(S^{\prime}).

\bullet

Finite character: If
a\in\mathsf{cl}(S)
, then
a\in\mathsf{cl}(S^{\prime})
,
for some finite
S^{\prime}\subseteq S
.

\bullet

Exchange axiom: If
a\in\mathsf{cl}(S\cup\{b\})
and
a\notin\mathsf{cl}(S)
, then
b\in\mathsf{cl}(S\cup\{a\}).

❍

Familiar facts from the elementary theory of vector spaces regarding the
notions of spanning, independence, basis, and dimension can be developed
within pregeometries (with similar proofs). More specifically, given
a pregeometry
(X,\mathsf{cl})
, consider the following dictionary:

\bullet

For
S\subseteq X
,
\mathsf{span}(S):=\mathsf{cl}(S)
.

\bullet

For
S\subseteq X
,
S
is
independent
if

a\notin\mathsf{cl}(S\backslash\{a\})
for all
a\in S
.

\bullet

A subset
B
of
X
is a
basis
if
\mathsf{span}(S)=X
and
S
is independent.

The following encapsulates the basic results concerning the above notions.

Theorem C.4
.

The statements below hold in every pregeometry
(X,\mathsf{cl}).

1
.

S
is a basis iff
\mathsf{span}(S)=X
and there is no

S^{\prime}\subsetneq S
such that
\mathsf{span}(S^{\prime})=X
.

2
.

If
S
is independent, then there is some basis
B
such that

S\subseteq B.

3
.

If
\mathsf{span}(S)=X
, then there is some basis
B
such that
B\subseteq S.

4
.

If
B
and
B^{\prime}
are bases, then
B
and
B^{\prime}
have
the same cardinality
(
referred to as the dimension of
(X,\mathsf{cl})
)
.

5
.

If
B
is a basis and
a\in X
, then there is a unique minimal finite subset
Z

of
B
such that
a\in\mathsf{span}(Z)
.

6
.

If
Z
and
Z^{\prime}
are subsets of a basis
B
, then
Z\subseteq Z^{\prime}
iff
\mathsf{span}(Z)\subseteq\mathsf{span}(Z^{\prime})
.

Note that if
B
is a basis for a pregeometry
(X,\mathsf{cl})
, then part (5) of the above theorem assures us that we can meaningfully define the support function

\mathsf{supp}_{(X,\mathsf{cl},B)}:\mathcal{P}(X)\rightarrow[B]^{<\omega},

by
\mathsf{supp}_{(X,\mathsf{cl},B)}(a)=Z
if
Z
is the unique minimal subset of
B
such that
a\in\mathsf{span}(Z)
.

The following result readily follows from Theorem
C.4
and the definitions involved.

Theorem C.5
.

Suppose
B
is a basis for a pregeometry
(X,\mathsf{cl})
. The
following statements are equivalent for
a\in X
and
Z\in[B]^{<\omega}.

1
.

Z=\mathsf{supp}_{(X,\mathsf{cl},B)}(a)
.

2
.

Z
is the minimum element of
[B]^{<\omega}
such that
a\in\mathsf{cl}(Z).

Putting the fact that
(\mathcal{K},\mathsf{acl}_{\mathcal{K}})
is a pregeometry together with the above, we obtain:

Theorem C.6
.

Suppose
\mathcal{K}
is a field, and
B
is a basis
23
23

23

In this context
B
is commonly referred to as a
transcendence basis/base
for
\mathcal{K}
.
for the pregeometry
(\mathcal{K},\mathsf{acl}_{\mathcal{K}})
.
The following statements are equivalent for
a\in\mathcal{K}
and
Z\in[B]^{<\omega}
:

1
.

Z
is a minimal element of
[B]^{<\omega}
such that
a\in\mathsf{acl}_{\mathcal{K}}(Z)
, in other words

Z=\mathsf{supp}_{(\mathcal{K},\mathsf{acl}_{\mathcal{K}},B)}(a)
.

2
.

Z
is the minimum element of
[B]^{<\omega}
such that
a\in\mathsf{acl}_{\mathcal{K}}(Z).

This concludes our review of the relevant basic facts about pregeometries and
their relationship to fields. We will now apply the above machinery to the field

\mathbb{F}=\mathsf{rc}(\hat{\mathbb{A}})=\mathsf{rc}(\hat{\mathbb{B}})
(see Remark
7.10
).

\bullet

In
what follows,
\mathfrak{X}:=\{\,\mathsf{X}_{q}\mathop{\mid}q\in\mathbb{Q}\}
, and for

Y\in[\mathbb{Q}]^{<\omega}
,

\mathfrak{X}_{Y}:=\{\,\mathsf{X}_{q}\mathop{\mid}q\in Y\}.

Theorem C.7
.

For every
a\in\mathbb{F}
there is a unique
Y\in[\mathbb{Q}]^{<\omega}
such that
a\in\mathsf{rc}(\hat{\mathbb{B}}(Y))=\mathsf{acl}_{\mathbb{F}}(\mathfrak{X}_{Y})
.

Proof.

\mathfrak{X}
is clearly a basis for
(\mathbb{F},\mathsf{acl}_{\mathbb{F}})
, so we can invoke Theorem
C.6
.
Note that since
\mathbb{F}
is real closed,
\mathsf{acl}_{\mathbb{F}}(\mathfrak{X}_{Y})=\mathsf{rc}(\hat{\mathbb{B}}(Y))
for all

Y\in[\mathbb{Q}]^{<\omega}
.
❑

Theorem C.8
.

\hat{\mathbb{B}}(Y)=\hat{\mathbb{B}}\cap\mathsf{rc}(\hat{\mathbb{B}}(Y))
for each
Y\in[\mathbb{Q}]^{<\omega}.

Proof.

The direction
\hat{\mathbb{B}}(Y)\subseteq\hat{\mathbb{B}}\cap\mathsf{rc}(\mathbb{B}(Y))
is straightforward. The other direction follows from the
fact that
\hat{\mathbb{B}}(Y)
is an integer part of
\mathsf{rc}(\hat{\mathbb{B}}(Y))
for
each
Y\in[\mathbb{Q}]^{<\omega}
(see Remark
7.15
).

More explicitly, suppose
b\in\hat{\mathbb{B}}\cap\mathsf{rc}(\hat{\mathbb{B}}(Y))
.
Then, since
\hat{\mathbb{B}}(Y)
is an integer part of
\mathsf{rc}(\hat{\mathbb{B}}(Y))
,
there is some
b^{\prime}\in\hat{\mathbb{B}}(Y)
such that
\left|b-b^{\prime}\right|<1
,
which implies
b=b^{\prime}
, thus
b\in\hat{\mathbb{B}}(Y)
.
❑

Thanks to Theorem
C.6
, we can meaningfully define the rank function for

b\in\hat{\mathbb{B}}
as follows:

\bullet

\mathsf{supp}_{\hat{\mathbb{B}}}(b)=\{q\in\mathbb{Q}\mathop{\mid}\mathsf{X}_{q}\in\mathsf{supp}_{(\mathbb{F},\mathsf{acl},\mathfrak{X}\}}(b)\}
.

Theorems
C.7
,
C.6
, and
C.8
readily imply the following characterization of the support function in
\hat{\mathbb{B}}
.

Theorem C.9
.

The following are equivalent for
b\in\hat{\mathbb{B}}
and
Y\in[\mathbb{Q}]^{<\omega}.

1
.

Y=\mathsf{supp}_{\hat{\mathbb{B}}}(b)
.

2
.

Y
is a minimal element of

[\mathbb{Q}]^{<\omega}
such that
a\in\hat{\mathbb{B}}(Y).

3
.

Y
is the minimum element of

[\mathbb{Q}]^{<\omega}
such that
a\in\hat{\mathbb{B}}(Y).

Remark C.10
.

Using Theorems
C.6
and
C.8
we can prove that
\mathbb{B}(Y)\subseteq\mathbb{B}(Y^{\prime})

implies
Y\subseteq Y^{\prime}

(
the converse is of course trivial
)
.
